„Polizei durchsucht mehrere Wohnungen wegen antisemitischer Hetze [Police search several residences because of anti-Semitic agitation],“ Zeit Online, November 21, 2023,\nhttps://www.zeit.de/gesellschaft/2023-11/bayern-razzia-antisemitismus-d…", "excerpt_sha256": "fda7528f5d2da6fd3d8da41a25ff6d095aedf93e2c9aa842587a6ba88debb349", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports all stated figures, the comparison with the prior two years, and the November 2023 raids involving 17 suspects."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0010-E009", "source_id": "SRC-bb0a509f84180c43", "url": "https://freedomhouse.org/country/germany/freedom-net/2024", "title": "Germany: Freedom on the Net 2024 Country Report", "publisher": "Freedom House", "raw_sha256": "311e1b42a7a16fa2c4f17bc1bde5ed207530a183746c600ece7af9f6da1ceab0", "fact_summary": "German surveillance law permits warrant-based source telecommunications monitoring and online searches, while intelligence services have broader powers; in July 2023 the Justice Ministry proposed narrowing police use of remote forensic software and prohibiting secret activation of cameras and microphones, but the proposal had not yet become law.", "question_ids": ["3B.5", "4B.9", "8B.2", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The reform proposal is directly dated July 2023; the underlying surveillance framework predates the period.", "ruler_attribution": "The Justice Ministry in Scholz's federal government proposed the restrictions; the source does not show a personal Scholz directive.", "limitations": ["The proposal's enactment and effectiveness are not established by the document.", "State-level and intelligence-service powers remain broader than the proposed federal police limits."], "start_unit": 65, "end_unit": 68, "locator": "extracted HTML block 65 through extracted HTML block 68", "exact_excerpt": ".\n8\nAndre Meister, “Immer mehr EU-Staaten gegen unverhältnismäßige Chatkontrolle [A growing number of EU countries against disproportionate chat control],“ Netzpolitik, November 23, 2023,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2023/internes-protokoll-immer-mehr-eu-staaten-g…\n.\n9\nMarkus Reuter, “EU-Datenschutzausschuss warnt weiterhin vor willkürlicher Überwachung [EU Data Protection Committee continues to warn against arbitrary surveillance],“ Netzpolitik, February 15, 2024,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2024/chatkontrolle-eu-datenschutzausschuss-warn…\n; Andre Meister, “EU-Staaten drehen sich bei Chatkontrolle im Kreis [EU countries are going round in circles when it comes to chat control],“ Netzpolitik, April 12, 2024,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2024/internes-protokoll-eu-staaten-drehen-sich-…\n.\n10\nAnna Biselli, “Freiwillige Chatkontrolle soll in die Verlängerung [Voluntary chat control to be extended],“ Netzpolitik, January 31, 2024,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2024/eu-parlament-freiwillige-chatkontrolle-sol…\n.\nC5\n1.00-6.00 pts\n0-6 pts\nDoes state surveillance of internet activities infringe on users’ right to privacy?\n2.00\n2\n6.00\n6\nArticle 10 of the Basic Law guarantees the privacy of letters, posts, and telecommunications. Furthermore, a groundbreaking 2008 BVerfG ruling established a new fundamental right regarding the “confidentiality and integrity of information technology systems” as part of the general right of personality under Article 2 of the Basic Law.\n1\n\nThese articles generally safeguard offline as well as online communication.\nThe 2008 BVerfG ruling permits covert online searches only in exigent circumstances.\n2\nBased on that ruling, the federal parliament passed a law in 2009 authorizing the BKA to conduct—with a warrant—covert online searches to prevent terrorist attacks and to employ other methods of covert data collection, including the installation of software on a suspect’s computer that intercepts their communications at the source.\n3\nGerman legislation generally distinguishes between two surveillance mechanisms referred to as remote forensic software (Staatstrojaner\n)\n: source telecommunication surveillance (Quellen–TKÜ) and online searches (Online-Durchsuchung). Using source telecommunication surveillance, ongoing communications can be accessed before or after they are encrypted. On the other hand, online searches provide intelligence agencies with the authority to secretly access all files, programs, and messages on a device, including by exploiting security vulnerabilities.\n4\nAmong other tools, German law enforcement and intelligence agencies have deployed Pegasus spyware, which is developed by the Israeli company NSO Group, since 2020.\n5\nThe use of surveillance technologies by authorities is permitted only in relation to certain criminal offenses, with different rules applying to law enforcement and intelligence agencies. Law enforcement agencies have access to both source telecommunication surveillance and online searches, provided that a judicial warrant is obtained. In contrast, intelligence services are generally only allowed to use source telecommunication surveillance, although they are not directly subject to direct oversight. In June 2021, legislative amendments permitted all 19 German intelligence services to utilize an “extended Quellen–TKÜ.”\n6\nThis allows for the monitoring of nonactive communications, including chats and text messages, following the authorization of a surveillance order and it is subject to the rules of source telecommunication surveillance. Consequently, this amendment—developed largely in response to right-wing extremism—has been criticized for blurring the regulatory line between source telecommunication surveillance and online searches.\n7\nArticle 10 of the Basic Law stipulates that limiting the secrecy of letters, posts, and telecommunications may only be ordered on grounds specified in law. These limitations are defined and regulated by the Act for Limiting the Secrecy of Letters, Posts, and Telecommunications (G-10 Act), which was originally enacted in 1968 and has since undergone several amendments. In accordance with the legislation, the Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution (BfV), the constitutional protection authorities of the federal states, the Military Counterintelligence Service (MAD), and the Federal Intelligence Service (BND) are legally permitted to deviate from Article 10 of the Basic Law under specified circumstances. However, targeted surveillance of individuals is only allowed if there is suspicion of specifically defined criminal offenses, such as treason. The G-10 Commission is tasked with determining whether the implementation of specific surveillance measures is permissible and necessary. Additionally, the commission decides whether the individuals affected must be informed following the termination of the surveillance. The Bundestag is responsible for monitoring the commission’s activities through an elected parliamentary oversight body. At the state level, the respective federal state commissions and parliaments are responsible for decision-making and control. The parliamentary oversight body reports annually on the implementation, type, and scale of the surveillance measures.\n8\nSurveillance conducted by intelligence services under the Act for Limiting the Secrecy of Letters, Posts, and Telecommunications (G-10 Act) has increased slightly. The most recent parliamentary oversight body report, published in 2024, presents data on surveillance under the G-10 Act conducted in 2021. Since 2016, 1,460 surveillance measures on individuals have been authorized, with 243 of these taking place in 2021. A total of 18,849 were targeted under these measures in 2021 compared to 6,897 individuals affected in 2020 and only 1,180 in 2019. The sharp increase is explained by a single BND measure, ongoing since 2018, targeting “Islamist” terrorism.\n9\nBND surveillance measures and their legal basis have been under criticism for several years. A 2016 law explicitly granted the BND permission to monitor domestic internet traffic so long as it targets non–German citizens.\n10\nNevertheless, the law has been scrutinized for its impact on the privacy of German internet users.\n11\nWhile the BND is mainly tasked with foreign intelligence collection, the law permits monitoring of all network traffic channeled through the Frankfurt-located DE–CIX—the world’s largest internet exchange point (IXP)—which would at least unintentionally affect communications by German citizens as well. Furthermore, press freedom groups argued that the law threatens the constitutionally protected work of foreign journalists reporting in Germany and outside the country\n12\n\nand, in January 2018, several nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) and foreign journalists filed a constitutional complaint.\n13\nIn May 2020, the Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) declared that the law was unconstitutional in response to the complaint. The BVerfG ruled that the law violated fundamental rights, and the German government's obligation to respect fundamental rights is not limited to German territory. Therefore, the government had to revise the law.\n14\nIn January 2022, an amended version of the BND Law took effect.\n15\nThe law expands the scope of BND activities and allows the interception of up to 30 percent of the transmission capacity of all global telecommunications networks.\n16\nMoreover, the legislation permits the BND to conduct online searches of foreigners abroad in addition to the previously permitted source telecommunication surveillance. While the law protects “individual communications of natural persons,” it allows the BND to collect and process various kinds of information, including inventory, traffic, and content data, enabling the monitoring of communications behavior, financial transaction data, and movement of individuals in Germany and abroad. The insufficient protection of journalists, criticized by the BVerfG, is regulated in an added paragraph. However, the Federal Data Protection Commissioner, opposition politicians, and legal observers have criticized vague exceptions in the protection clause.\n17\nIn January 2023, RSF filed a new complaint to the BVerfG, saying protections for journalists remained too weak.\n18\nHowever, this constitutional complaint was rejected in February 2024, due to insufficient reasoning.\n19\nTelecommunications interception by state authorities for criminal prosecutions is regulated by the criminal code and may only be employed for the prosecution of serious crimes for which specific evidence exists and when other, less intrusive investigative methods are likely to fail. In August 2017, the federal parliament enacted the “Law for More Effective and More Practical Criminal Proceedings.” An extensive list of criminal offenses allows police and investigative authorities to intercept phone calls. The law expands the scope of the list to allow the use of remote forensic software on suspects’ mobile phones, tablets, and computers to enable monitoring of written and spoken text and the copying of data.\n20\nMoreover, the legislation delineates specific criminal offenses that permit the use of source telecommunication surveillance and online searches. The use of remote forensic software for criminal prosecution is regulated by this law, while the law allows the states to regulate preventive hacking in their respective police laws.\n21\nIn line with legislation, several state laws have been enacted that grant police vastly expanded powers to access communications.\n22\nCritics consider the 2017 legislation unconstitutional due to its expansive scope and long list of applicable offenses, claiming it contradicts the 2008 BVerfG ruling on Article 2 of the Basic Law.\n23\nAt the national level, the Society for Civil Rights (GFF) filed a constitutional complaint against the law in 2018.\n24\nIn April 2023, GFF was given the opportunity to make representations in the proceedings.\n\n25\nNevertheless, the BVerfG had not reached a verdict as of the end of the coverage period.\n26\nThe constitutional complaint against the police law in Baden-Württemberg was rejected in 2021; in its reasoning, the BVerfG derived a state duty to protect the information technology (IT) security of all Germans. Therefore, the state must weigh the exploitation of security flaws in its deployment of remote forensic software against the information security interests of the public.\n27\nIn 2022, 4,981 telecommunication interceptions were ordered, most frequently for drug crimes.\n28\nIn addition, the surveillance of IT devices by means of the remote forensic software and source telecommunications surveillance, was ordered in 94 proceedings and carried out in 49 cases.\n29\nFinally, six online searches were ordered, of which four were actually carried out.\n30\nIn comparison, in 2021, 5,174 telecommunication interceptions, 23 instances of source telecommunications surveillance and 9 online searches were conducted.\n31\nIn July 2023, the Ministry of Justice presented a bill to place some restrictions on the use of remote forensic software for police authorities. Currently, 44 different crimes permit the use of source telecommunication surveillance and 33 particularly serious crimes permit the use of online searches. With the amendment, the 33 crimes will apply to both the use of source telecommunication surveillance and online searches. In addition, the secret activation of cameras and microphones will be explicitly prohibited in the amendment.\n32\nIn Hesse in 2018, legislation establishing law enforcement’s right to use remote forensic software also allowed agencies to conduct automated analysis of personal data. Hesse uses Gotham, a predictive policing tool produced by the US–based company Palantir to analyze different data sources, including internal police databases and Facebook data.\n33\nNorth Rhine–Westphalia purchased similar software in 2020, Bavaria in 2022, and Hamburg reportedly planned to implement it in 2019.\n34\nThese tools have been criticized for using discriminatory data bases and producing mass errors.\n35\nIn February 2023, the BVerfG ruled that bills in Hesse and Hamburg that legalized data mining and predictive policing were unconstitutional.\n36\nFollowing the ruling, most federal states’ ministries have declared their intention to refrain from utilizing Gotham or analogous software in the future. Nevertheless, Bavaria, North Rhine–Westphalia, and Hesse have indicated that they intend to continue using the software, while Berlin, Hamburg, and Thuringia were evaluating its potential deployment as of the end of the coverage period.\n37", "excerpt_sha256": "2a04c3c282216dc175d0f103c3e4c8096c6f26c8019af266ab34469c9790ea21", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports warrant-based law-enforcement surveillance, broader intelligence-service powers, the July 2023 bill, and its uncompleted status."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0010-E010", "source_id": "SRC-bb0a509f84180c43", "url": "https://freedomhouse.org/country/germany/freedom-net/2024", "title": "Germany: Freedom on the Net 2024 Country Report", "publisher": "Freedom House", "raw_sha256": "311e1b42a7a16fa2c4f17bc1bde5ed207530a183746c600ece7af9f6da1ceab0", "fact_summary": "In February 2023 the Federal Constitutional Court ruled that data-mining and predictive-policing laws in Hesse and Hamburg were unconstitutional; However, government institutions and the business sector have been targeted by cyberattacks.\n1\nIn June 2024, after the coverage period, the Christian Democratic Party (CDU) experienced a cyberattack ahead of the European Parliament elections. The Ministry of the Interior said it was likely launched by a serious actor.\n2\nIn March 2022, after the start of the Kremlin’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, the Federal Office for Information Security (BSI) noted that cybersecurity threats had intensified, especially in terms of ransomware and blackmailing.\n3\nThe German domestic intelligence services have warned about attacks by Russian hacktivists, who have gained access to data belonging to some members of the federal states’ parliaments through phishing attacks.\n4\nIn its May 2024 report on cybercrime, the Ministry of the Interior stated that the threat level remained high, explicitly citing Russian actors as one of the main reasons.\n5\nSeveral cyberattacks by pro-Russian groups occurred between 2022 and 2024, including an attack on a satellite provider in March 2022 that caused a brief outage of German wind turbines.\n6\nRussian cyberattacks consisted mainly of distributed denial-of-service (DDoS) attacks with limited damage aimed at undermining public trust in the state's ability to protect its citizens.\n7\nFor instance, in March 2024, a recording of an internal meeting of Bundeswehr officers on arms supplies to Ukraine was broadcast on Russian state television.\n8\nIn addition, in January 2023, several email accounts belonging to board members of the Social Democratic Party (SPD) were hacked, which in May 2024 the German government attributed to a Russian military intelligence unit.\n9\nIn January 2023, the government published key points for a new law for the Central Office for Information Technology in the Security Sector (ZITiS), which provides cybersecurity support to federal agencies. The law does not explicitly prevent ZITiS from helping security agencies conduct surveillance. The opposition Left Party also criticized the lack of parliamentary oversight of ZITiS in the draft.\n10\n\n1\nNicolai Kwasniewski, “Ein Hack, eine versetzte Schweißnaht – fatale Folgen [A hack, a shifted weldseam – fatal consequences],“ Spiegel Online, November 30, 2017,\nhttps://www.spiegel.de/wirtschaft/unternehmen/cyberangriffe-so-gefaehrd…\n.\n2\nFlorian Rudolph, “Cyberangriff auf die CDU: 'Wir befinden uns in einem hybriden Krieg' [Cyber attack on the CDU: 'We are in a hybrid war'],“ SWR, June 14, 2024,\nhttps://www.swr.de/swraktuell/radio/cyberangriff-auf-die-cdu-wir-befind…\n; The overall recognition rate at first instance\n     stood at 68.6% (i.e., 21.6% refugee status, 36.2% subsidiary protection and 10.9% humanitarian\n     protection). It reached 99.9% for Syrians, 98.7% for Afghans, but only 17.8% for Turkish nationals\n     and 33.2% for Iraqis. Other nationalities such as Georgians, North Macedonians or Moldovans were\n     nearly all rejected with a rejection rate around 99%. An additional 5,881 persons were granted\n     international protection by Courts at second instance until the end of August 2023. The number of\n     pending cases at the BAMF, which had more than doubled between the end of 2020 and 2021 from\n     52,056 to 108,064, increased again from 136,448 at the end of 2022 to 239,614 at the end of 2023.\n     Until the end of August 2023, the rate of successful appeals has dropped from 37% to approx. 25 %\n     in merit decisions.\n\nAsylum Procedure\n\nv Act on the acceleration of asylum court proceedings and asylum procedures. The reform\n  entered into force on 1 January 2023. The most important changes of the reform include:\n       o The introduction of independent counselling for asylum seekers, instead of the state-run\n          counselling which was introduced in 2019 (seeInformation for asylum seekers and access to\n          NGOs and UNHCR)\n       o Changes to the rules for personal interviews: an additional ground for dispensing with the\n          interview was introduced when the BAMF is of the opinion that the foreigner is unable to\n          attend a hearing due to permanent circumstances beyond their control and the possibility of\n          conducting video interviews (see Personal interview)\n       o The provisions on time limits for the asylum procedure were changed in order to closely mirror\n          the relevant provisions of the EU Asylum Procedure Directive (see Regular procedure -\n          General (scope, time limits))\n       o A change in the rules for onward appeals (Revision) to the Federal Administrative Court,\n          according to which the latter can now also adjudicate on the facts of the case, rather than\n          merely on points of law (see Regular procedure - Onward appeal).\n       o Complete revision of the grounds for cessation of the residence permit and substantial\n          amendments to the grounds of revocation of the residence permit (see Cessation and review\n          of protection status).\n  In contrast to the rules introduced by the legal reform concerning independent counselling, the\n  Highest Administrative Court decided in March 2023 that local authorities are not required to provide\n  regular access for NGOs and welfare associations to reception centres.15 Only where independent\n  counselling is explicitly requested by the applicant, must access be granted. The repercussions of\n  this and whether this leads to more difficulties in accessing legal aid need to be examined in practice.\n  In general, the introduction of independent counselling falls short of its goals due to delays in\n  implementation, practical obstacles for NGOs to access the arrival centres, insufficient funding for\n  2023 and 2024 and lack of reliable long-term funding strategies from the state (see Information for\n  asylum seekers and access to NGOs and UNHCR).\n\nv Continued reading out of mobile devices in asylum procedures: The Act to Improve Removals\n  introduced a new norm that allows the authorities to read out mobile devices. Legal scholars criticise\n  the provision as unconstitutional and the Federal Administrative Court has already emphasised that\n  it must be applied only ultima ratio (see Lodging the application).\n\nv    Increased border controls and suspected illegal push backs: The Federal Government has\n     introduced further ‘temporary’ stationary border controls on the borders with Poland, the Czech\n     Republic and Switzerland during 2023. They have regularly been prolonged and are still in place.\n     With these increased border controls, a significant decrease in asylum claims on the border has been\n\n\n\n15\n       Highest Administrative Court (BVerwG), Decision 1 C 40.21, 28 March 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/480lN0o, para. 27f.\n\n\n                                                     16\n\nobserved while the number of people arriving is further increasing. Some stakeholders suspect illegal\n     push backs by the Federal Police (see Access to the territory and push backs).\n\nv    Breaches of church asylum: In light of a hardening political climate, the authorities have breached\n     into church premises where persons were housed in the context of church asylum in 2023. Due to\n     strong protests, they however did ultimately not transfer the asylum seekers concerned (see Dublin\n     - Procedure).\n\nv Humanitarian admission programme for Afghan nationals: On 17 October 2022, the Federal\n  Government launched a federal admissions programme which had been announced in the coalition\n  agreement of 2021.16 Following false accusations of misuse, the admission programme was put on\n  hold in March 2023. It was resumed in June 2023, but a new screening mechanism was introduced\n  which includes data checks and specific security interviews (see Differential treatment of specific\n  nationalities in the procedure). NGOs criticise that the temporary suspension and the introduction of\n  the additional screening procedure caused further delays and that the programme does not live up to\n  the originally envisaged admission scheme.\n\nv Unaccompanied minors:\n     o Due to overburdening of local authorities in 2023, unaccompanied children often did not have\n        access to special care such as psychological assistance and to schooling (see Legal\n        representation of unaccompanied minors)\n     o Age assessment has been repeatedly criticised for providing a large margin of discretion to\n        the authorities (Identification – Age assessment of unaccompanied children).\n\nv Safe Country concept: In 2023, Georgia and Moldova were added to the list of safe countries of\n  origin. NGOs criticise this decision. They claim that, in these countries, minorities such as Roma and\n  LGBTIQ+ members face discrimination and that there has been a backlash to democracy and the\n  rule of law (see Safe country of origin).\n\nv    Removal ban for Iran: The federal removal ban for Iran has been lifted as of January 1st, 2024.\n     However, over the course of 2023, four persons were removed to Iran despite the ban.\n\nv Key jurisprudence on subsequent applications: The CJEU decided in February 2024 that its\n  rulings can qualify as ‘new elements’ under the APD.17 This means that subsequent applications\n  should be declared admissible if CJEU court rulings strongly suggest the existence of a ‘new legal\n  situation’ even if the CJEU ruling only concerns the interpretation of EU law. The ruling came as a\n  result of a preliminary ruling requested by the Administrative Court of Sigmaringen in 2022, asking\n  whether the two conditions for the admissibility of a subsequent application are compatible with the\n  EU Asylum Procedures Directive (APD).18 In addition, the CJEU ruled that a temporary return to the\n  country of origin has no impact on further applications as to whether these further applications are\n  subsequent applications19 (see Subsequent applications).\n\nReception conditions\n\nv Reintroduction of emergency shelters: Due to the increasing arrivals of protection seekers,\n  several municipalities, especially larger cities, have had to reintroduce emergency shelters. Without\n  any change in 2023, especially in larger cities, exhibition grounds and/or made-up tent facilities are\n  used to host asylum seekers. The conditions in these emergency shelters are below standards.\n\n16\n      For further details, see AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2021, April 2022, available at\n      https://bit.ly/3XnN7RS, 11.\n17\n      Ibid.\n18\n      Art. 33(2)d and 40(2) recast APD. Number of staff and nature of the first instance authority\n\n     Name in English              Number of staff          Ministry responsible            Is there any political\n                                                                                      interference possible by the\n                                                                                         responsible Minister with\n                                                                                          the decision making in\n                                                                                       individual cases by the first\n                                                                                            instance authority?\n                                 8,447 positions\n     Federal Office for                                     Federal Ministry of\n                                 (7,355 full-time\n      Migration and                                          the Interior and                    Yes     No\n                               positions in various\n     Refugees (BAMF)                                           Community\n                              asylum departments)\n\nSource: Information provided by the BAMF, up to date as of 15 December 2023.\n\nThe BAMF is responsible for examining applications for international protection and competent to take\ndecisions at first instance.\n\nThe BAMF has branch offices in all Federal States. As of February 2024, the BAMF website lists a total\nof 58 branch offices.33 The branch offices process the asylum procedures, but also carry out additional\ntasks (for instance, they function as contact points for authorities and organisations active in the\nintegration of foreign nationals, while some branch offices work exclusively on Dublin cases). Branch\noffices are assigned specific countries of origin, whereas the main countries of origin are processed in the\n\n\n\n33\n        BAMF, Locations, available at: https://bit.ly/3dFTd8w.\n\n\n                                                          23\n\nmajority of branch offices.34 In cooperation with the Federal States, the BAMF manages a distribution\nsystem for asylum seekers known as Initial Distribution of Asylum Seekers (Erstverteilung der\nAsylbegehrenden, EASY) system, which allocates places according to a quota system known as\n“Königsteiner Schlüssel” (see Asylum Act). The quota is based on the size and the economic strength of\nthe Federal States in which the centres are located. Furthermore, the system takes into account which\nbranch office of the BAMF deals with an asylum seeker's country of origin.\n\nAs of November 2023, the BAMF had 7.355 positions or “full-time job equivalents” working on various\naspects of asylum (meaning that the actual number of staff is much higher, since many of these positions\nare shared by people working part-time). Since the office is responsible for several other tasks on top of\nthe asylum procedure (e.g., research, integration, migration for reasons other than asylum and return\npolicies), not all staff members are working in the area of asylum. The overall number of positions is 8,138\naccording to the Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Community.35\n\nThe government provided the following numbers for positions in the relevant departments as of November\n2023:36\n        v asylum department (excluding revocation and Dublin procedures): 2,327.9 full-time\n          equivalents;\n        v revocation procedures: 105.2 full-time equivalents;\n        v procedures (appeal procedures, representation of the BAMF in court): 376.0 full-time\n          equivalents;\n        v quality management: 141.0 full-time equivalents;\n        v Dublin-procedures: 338.4 full-time equivalents.\n\nThe BAMF also has special officers for security issues. They are responsible for a whole range of issues\nand should be involved in asylum procedures whenever indications arise for instances of ‘extremism,\nterrorism, criminality, human trafficking, war crimes, crimes against humanity and smuggling of human\nbeings’.37 The special officers for security issues act as contact points between the BAMF and other\nauthorities, but they do not necessarily take part in interviews or take over responsibility of particular\nasylum procedures.\n\nQuality\n\nThe quality of BAMF asylum decisions has been much debated in recent years given the high number of\nappeals filed at the courts, but also because of “scandals” which prompted extensive media coverage in\n2017 and 2018.38 This was related, in part, to the high increase in personnel in 2015 and 2016 – likely\ndue to the spike in asylum applications –, accompanied by shortened training phases, with some decision-\nmakers not having received relevant training. As a result, the BAMF has undertaken several changes to\nthe training provided to decision-makers and to the quality assurance procedures since 2017. As of 2018,\nshort summaries of interview transcripts and notice letters are checked by a second employee. Randomly\nselected cases are subject to a more thorough quality control by the BAMF’s quality assurance division.\nIn addition, the BAMF also has a division for ‘Operative management of asylum procedures and\nintegration’ which ‘analyses developments and trends so that it is possible to recognise and react to a\nneed to act for management at an early date’, according to the BAMF.39 In particular, the decision-making\npractices of the different branch offices are monitored and branch offices with significant deviations from\n\n34\n      A list of all countries of origin and the allocated branch offices is available on the website of the Refugee\n      Council of Lower Saxony (up to date as of March 20221): https://bit.ly/3WJ0eg1.\n35\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community, ‘Bundesamt für Migration und Flüchtlinge’, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/3qTH0qt.\n36\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/9933, 28 December 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/42NJDMa, 30-31.\n37\n      BAMF, Entscheiderbrief (newsletter for decision-makers) 4/2020, 1.\n38\n      For more detailed information, see AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2019, July 2020,\n      available at: https://bit.ly/34so09M, 20-21.\n39\n      BAMF, Procedure management and quality assurance, 28 November 2018, available at: http://bit.ly/3DxsTgJ.\n\n\n                                                        24", "excerpt_sha256": "8e89c9fc9799b9e48de2bb9d933ba0310ce8d043d23aba81d6af8ac40819ba38", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not support the claim that monitoring results were not automatically public."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R01-E013", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; NGOs argued that the controls caused refusals of entry and denied some people access to BAMF assessment, while a police union said they shifted routes rather than reducing irregular immigration.", "question_ids": ["3B.5", "3B.8", "4B.9", "8B.6", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The controls were introduced and extended during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The controls were introduced by the Federal Government; the source does not establish Scholz’s personal authorship or decision process.", "limitations": ["The source reports stakeholder assessments and does not establish the legality or causal effect of the controls conclusively."], "start_unit": 28, "end_unit": 28, "locator": "page 28", "exact_excerpt": "refusal of entry (Zurückweisung); out of these, 4,489 persons were removed to Austria.60 The general\nnumber is higher than for the first half of 2022 (8,986) and probably due to increased border controls.\n\nGermany has regularly re-introduced border controls at its borders with Austria since 2015. On 16 October\n2023, controls were also introduced at the border with Poland, the Czech Republic and Switzerland and\nwere extended again in December 2023 and March 2024.61 The agreement with Switzerland also includes\ncontrols on Swiss territory. This allows the authorities to prevent people from entering German territory\nand they are then consequently not responsible for any asylum requests.62 The prolongations occurred\ndespite a ruling of the CJEU of 26 April 2022 in which the court states that border controls cannot exceed\na duration of 6 months unless there is a new threat justifying a renewed introduction of controls for another\nsix months maximum.63 The extension has been continually criticised by NGOs such as PRO ASYL, who\nargue that controls lead to refusals of entry of would-be asylum seekers in Germany, who are denied\naccess to an assessment by the BAMF of whether Germany might be responsible for handling their\nasylum application.64 A representative of the union of police officers repeatedly criticised the extensions,\non the grounds that they do not reduce irregular immigration but rather shift routes to other land borders.65\n\nIn 2018, following a heated political debate, a new procedure was introduced which enables the Federal\nPolice to refuse entry at the border and send persons back to Greece and Spain within 48 hours if they\nhave previously applied for asylum there.66 This procedure is based on administrative regulations and\nspecial administrative readmission agreements with the two countries. These returns are therefore not\nbased on the Dublin Regulation, but on a refusal of entry under the (national) notion of ‘safe third countries’\nin combination with administrative arrangements concluded with other EU Member States. Since 2019, it\nwas only applied to persons found at the Austrian-German border, as this was the only border where\ncontrols continue to take place. While being heavily debated in 2018, the introduction of the new\nprocedure had little effect in practice: between August 2018 and May 2021, only 50 persons were returned\n(46 returns to Greece and 4 to Spain) on the basis of the readmission agreements with these countries.67\nWhile no refusals of entry were carried out between May 2021 and the end of 2021 according to the\nFederal Police,68 two persons were returned to Spain each year in 2022 and 2023 and none to Greece.69\nTherefore, the political debate over the return procedures at the border, which had even triggered a\ngovernment crisis in 2018, has been described as ‘absurd’ in retrospect.70\n\n\n\n\n60\n      Ibid, 8.\n61\n      Dw.de, Innenministerin Faeser verlängert Grenzüberwachung, 8 December 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/48iodYA; European Commission, ‘Temporary Reintroduction of Border Control’, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3HLwSGd.\n62\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/8274, 07 September 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/49GtKZX, 21.\n63\n      CJEU. the court held that the practice interfered with the fundamental right to confidentiality and integrity of information-technology systems.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "3B.5", "4B.4", "4B.9", "7B.2", "8B.8"], "polarity": "exculpatory", "period_fit": "The judgment was issued during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The finding concerns BAMF practice and judicial correction; The obtained information can only be used to establish\nidentity or country of origin, and not for other purposes during the asylum procedure.125 The types of data\nthat are used as indications for country of origin or identity are geolocation data, the countries to which\nthe majority of phone calls are made, the language of communication, the countries in which the saved\ncontacts are located, or the domain host country of frequently used websites.126\nIf an applicant refuses to hand out their smartphone, the BAMF considers the application to be withdrawn\nand ends the asylum procedure.127 For subsequent applications, failure to hand out one’s smartphone\ncan be used as a ground to withdraw material benefits, as these can be reduced for applicants who fail\nto cooperate with authorities (see Reduction or withdrawal of reception conditions). However, reading out\nof smartphones is only done in these cases when the BAMF intends to conduct an interview with the\napplicant.128\n\nThe practice of screening applicants’ smartphones was ruled illegal by the Federal Administrative Court\non 16 February 2023, after the Gesellschaft für Freiheitsrechte (GFF, an NGO focused on strategic\n\n118\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 10 March 2022.\n119\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023.\n120\n      For an extensive overview of data collection and management In the German asylum procedure see Janne\n      Grote, Accurate, timely, interoperable? rights) filed several lawsuits.129 The court ruled that the screening\ninterferes with the fundamental right to guarantee the confidentiality and integrity of information\ntechnology systems and that it is not lawful when less severe means are available to the BAMF to\nestablish applicants’’ identity, such as other certificates and documents (e. g. marriage certificates),\nregister comparisons and inquiries with the translator about linguistic abnormalities. Nevertheless, the\nFederal Government has passed a law for ‘improved removal’ in January 2024 that shall enable authorities\nto read out mobile devices and thus disregards the ruling of the High Court.130 The law has been heavily\ncriticised by NGOs131 as infringing upon people’s fundamental rights of privacy and informational\nautonomy.\n\nLanguage or dialect detection software can also only be used when no identity documents are provided,\nand only for applicants older than 14 years.132 Applicants have to speak into a telephone, and a speech\nanalysis software then produces a report on the languages or dialects detected and the probability that\nthese were indeed the spoken languages or dialects. As for smartphone screening, the results can never\nbe used as proof or identity or country of origin, but merely as indications which will be raised during the\npersonal interview in cases where the reports contradict the information given by the applicant. According\nto BAMF internal guidelines, as of January 2023 speech recognition is used for the main Arabic dialects\n(Maghrebian, Egyptian, Iraqi, Levantine and Gulf) and for Dari, Pashto and Persian.133 As of August 2022,\nthe detection rate for these languages and dialects was around 80% for Arabic dialects, ca. 73% for Dari\nand ca. 77% for Pashto.134 Such a speech and dialect recognition software has been used for 29,632\ncases in 2022 and for 22,947 cases from January until end of June 2023. The recognition rate for Arabic\ndialects rose to 87 % in 2023.135\n\nThe use of language detection software has been subject to criticism by NGOs and the opposition parties,\nwho claim that detection tools can be dangerous especially when asylum officers are overworked and\nthus more likely to rely on them as facts, rather than falsifiable indications. The software has been found\nto perform poorly especially for Arabic dialects in countries with a high number of local languages, such\nas Yemen or Sudan. In addition, the amount of training data for the artificial intelligence varies significantly\nbetween languages, leading to likely more accurate predictions for some languages than others.136\nFurthermore, while the BAMF has announced that a scientific study would accompany the introduction of\nthe language detection system, this has not yet happened.137\n\n\n\n\n129\n      Federal Administrative Court, Case 1 C 19.21, 16 February 2023. However, no 2023 complaint data were available, and earlier interpreter misconduct decisions had not triggered reassessment of affected cases.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.4", "3B.7", "4B.4", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The standards were current in April 2023, while some cited complaint and misconduct figures predated 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "These were federal administrative safeguards operating under the Scholz government; the source does not identify Olaf Scholz as personally directing them.", "limitations": ["No evidence is provided about Scholz's personal involvement, funding decisions, or review of the safeguards.", "The report does not quantify 2023 enforcement outcomes."], "start_unit": 47, "end_unit": 48, "locator": "page 47 through page 48", "exact_excerpt": "Video interpretation\n\nThe BAMF introduced the possibility of videoconferences for interpretation in 2016. This practice was\ncodified through the Act on the acceleration of asylum court proceedings and asylum procedures.224 The\nprovision allows for video interpretation ‘in suitable cases’ and ‘exceptionally’,225 indicating that, as for the\ninterview itself, interpretation in presence retains priority over video interpretation. In these cases,\ninterpreters sit in a different branch office than the one in which the interview is taking place or participate\nvia a so-called ‘interpretation-hub’, ensuring that all transmission is via a secure internal network. Video\ninterpretation is regarded as complementary to in-person interpretation. The BAMF internal guidelines\napply a relatively low threshold for this to be the case, however, by stating that video interpretation can\nbe used when there is an objective reason, such as a more efficient or flexible allocation of interpreters\ncost efficiency reasons, a shortage of interpreters in a certain area or for rare languages with few\ninterpreters. All countries of origin are in principle considered suitable for video interpretation, including\nwhen the applicant is considered vulnerable. However, special officers need to be included in the decision\nwhen it concerns unaccompanied minors, victims of gendered violence, torture, human trafficking or\ntraumatised persons.226 Video interpretation does not require consent by the applicant.227\n\nNo statistics on the use of video conferencing for interpretation were available for the years 2023 and\n2022 at the time of writing of this report. Video conferencing was used in 1,019 interviews in 2021 and\n1,359 interviews in 2020, compared to around 2,500 interviews in 2019.228 Thus, the Covid-19 outbreak\ndid not lead to more use of video interpretation. According to the BAMF, this is because distancing\nmeasures and contact avoidance were also implemented in the interpretation hub, leading to an overall\nlower number of interviews.229\n\nQuality of interpretation\n\nFollowing discussions about the quality of translations during interviews, the BAMF has revised the\nprocedures for the deployment of interpreters since 2017. For example, a new online training programme\nwas established.230 Both experienced and newly assigned interpreters are now required to complete the\ntraining programme. Apart from basic information on the asylum procedure and general communication\nskills, several training modules deal with specifics of the asylum interview such as the ‘role of the\ninterpreter during the interview’ or ‘handling psychological burden caused by asylum seekers’ traumatic\nbackgrounds. Interpreters further need advanced German language skills (level C1 of the Common\nEuropean Framework of Reference for Languages). Moreover, the BAMF established a system for\ncomplaint management in the context of interpretation at the BAMF in 2017.231 The complaint\nmanagement system was revised in 2020 and involves a multi-stage procedure at the end of which a\ntermination of contractual relations with the interpreter is possible.232\n\nIn addition, the BAMF has published standards for interpretation in the asylum procedure including a new\ncode of conduct which replaces which replace an earlier code of conduct adopted in 2017.233 According\nto this document, interpreters at the BAMF must not only have knowledge of their respective interpretation\n\n224\n      Official Gazette I no. Nr. 56 (2022) of 28 December 2022, 2817.\n225\n      Section 17(3) Asylum Act.\n226\n      See BAMF, Dienstanweisung Asyl (internal directive for asylum procedures), version of January 2023,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/3J5jPTA, 105.\n227\n      See BAMF, Dienstanweisung Asyl (internal directive for asylum procedures), version of January 2023, 111,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/3J5jPTA, 104-107.\n228\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 10 March 2022 and 8 April 2022.\n229\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 8 April 2022.\n230\n      BAMF, Dolmetschen und Übersetzen für das BAMF, 17 November 2022, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3HngsXd.\n231\n      BAMF, ‘Online-Videotraining für Sprachmittler gestartet’, 28 September 2017, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/2oWwbTH.\n232\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 10 March 2022.\n233\n      BAMF, Standards für das Dolmetschen im Asylverfahren, April 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3wjM4tz.\n\n\n                                                       47\n\nlanguage, but also show knowledge and qualifications in interpretation skills and in the asylum procedure\nand dealing with authorities. They must commit to five principles that are spelled out in more detail in the\nguidelines. These are “completeness and accuracy”, “transparency”, “all-party impartiality”, “professional\nintegrity” and “confidentiality”. In cases of repeated or serious violations of the standards or the code of\nconduct, the BAMF can decide to terminate the contract with an interpreter.234 Between 2017 and April\n2018, more than 2,100 interpreters were declared unfit for further interpretation assignments by the BAMF,\nmost of them apparently due to insufficient language skills. In 30 cases, interpreters were declared unfit\nbecause they were found to be in breach of the code of conduct.235 However, no re-assessment of the\ndecisions where these interpreters were involved has taken place.236 In 2022, the BAMF received 77\nnotifications via its complaint management system that were classified as complaints.237 Between 2017\nand February 2022, a total of 926 complaints were signalled to the BAMF via the same system.238 No\ninformation for the year 2023 was available as of April 2024.\n\nThe qualification requirements and pay for interpreters also vary between interviews at the BAMF and\ncourt hearings: whereas in court, interpreters must take an oath to accurately reflect the applicants’\nposition, this is not the case for interviews conducted with the BAMF or the Border Police. Reportedly,\ntaking oath in Court proceedings results in better translation services and cases being taken ‘more\nseriously’.239 Interpreters at court are, however, also generally paid more than interpreters contracted by\nthe BAMF – as of January 2023, the hourly rate for interpretation in courts is EUR 85,240 whereas the\nBAMF negotiates hourly rates for interpretation assignments which may vary according to individual levels\nof qualification. German courts, depending on the Federal land where they are located, may require higher\nlevels of qualifications than the BAMF.\n\n           1.3.2    Transcript of the interview\n\nThe transcript of the interview consists of a summary of questions and answers (i.e. it is not a verbatim\ntranscript) and is only available in German. The interpreter present during the personal interview is also\nresponsible for translating the transcript back to the applicant in oral form. The applicant has the right to\ncorrect mistakes or misunderstandings. By signing the transcript, the applicant confirms that they have\nhad the opportunity to present all the important details of the case, that there were no communication\nproblems and that the transcript was read back in the applicant's language. Video recordings of interviews\ndo not take place.\n\nIn spite of this, alleged mistakes in the transcript frequently give rise to disputes at later stages of the\nasylum procedure. For instance, doubts about the credibility of asylum seekers are often based on their\nstatements as they appear in the transcript. However, it is possible that the German wording of the\ntranscript reflects mistakes or misunderstandings which were caused by the translation. As mentioned\nabove, the transcript is usually translated orally once more at the end of the session by the same\ninterpreter who has been present during the interview as well. On this occasion, it is possible that\ninterpreters repeat the mistakes they made during the interview and it is thus impossible for the asylum\nseeker to identify errors in the German transcript which result from the interpreters' misunderstandings or\n\n\n\n234\n      BAMF, Standards für das Dolmetschen im Asylverfahren, April 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3wjM4tz, 1.\n235\n      Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 19/1631, 13 April 2018, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/2F2kvqq, 40-41.\n236\n      PRO ASYL, ‘Stellungnahme von PRO ASYL zum Antrag für ein umfassendes Qualitätsmanagement beim\n      Bundesamt für Migration und Flüchtlinge (BT-Drs. 19/4853) sowie zum Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Änderung\n      des Asylgesetzes zur Beschleunigung von Verfahren durch erweiterte Möglichkeit der Zulassung von\n      Rechtsmitteln (BT-Drs. 19/1319) 21’, available in German at: https://bit.ly/34Ge2Sy.\n237\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023.\n238\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 10 March 2022. During the first half of 2023, the federal government reported 47 Dublin transfers involving police use of physical restraints.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.2", "3B.4", "3B.8", "6B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The restraint figure covers the first half of 2023; the procedural rules were in force during the period.", "ruler_attribution": "These were statutory and administrative practices under the federal government, implemented by police, BAMF, and courts; personal responsibility of Scholz is not demonstrated.", "limitations": ["The source does not specify whether the restraints were lawful or excessive in each of the 47 cases.", "The legal-assistance assessment is reported as a practical concern, not a court finding applicable to every case."], "start_unit": 62, "end_unit": 65, "locator": "page 62 through page 65", "exact_excerpt": "ended.332 In Schwerin (Mecklenburg-Vorpommern), specialised police forces broke into an apartment\nowned by the church in December 2023 to deport two adult sons of a family of six from Afghanistan who\nhad been given assurances by the German government that they would get a right to enter Germany, but\nultimately had to flee via Iran and Spain since the visa procedure was taking too long. The transfer to\nSpain was halted after protests.333 In November 2023, the government of North Rhine Westphalia issued\ninternal guidelines in which it clarified that foreigners’ authorities cannot proceed with a transfer unless\nthe BAMF has clearly stated that it will not apply the sovereignty clause and explicitly orders the\ntransfer.334\n\nWithdrawal of benefits and detention\n\n‘Absconding’ from the Dublin procedure also has repercussions on Reduction or withdrawal of reception\nconditions, in that when the failure to transfer a person can be attributed to their behaviour, they are only\nentitled to reduced benefits.335 In cases of church asylum, according to a ruling by the administrative court\nof Bremen, persons who leave the district assigned to them by local authorities in order to find sanctuary\nin a church are no longer entitled to social benefits for asylum seekers.336 Absconding can also constitute\na ground for ordering Detention.337\n\nPractices as to detention before and during the Dublin procedure vary among the Federal States. Not all\nFederal States differentiate between Dublin transfers and removals to countries of origin in their detention\nstatistics. Among those which do collect and segregate the data, between 1.5% and 50% of all Dublin\ntransfers involved a form of detention in 2020.338 If asylum seekers have already accessed the regular\nprocedure, they must not be detained for the duration of the procedure. However, detention may be\nimposed once an application has been rejected as ‘inadmissible’ because another country was found to\nbe responsible for the asylum procedure, there is a risk of absconding and the removal order issued as\na result of the inadmissibility decision becomes enforceable. In this case, the legal basis for ordering and\nprolongation of detention is the same as for other forms of detention pending removal. This implies that\ncertain preconditions for the lawfulness of detention have to be fulfilled: in particular, any placing into\ncustody under these circumstances should generally be ordered in advance by a judge, since it does not\nconstitute a provisional arrest which may be authorised by a court at a later stage. However, a judge\nshould generally not issue a detention order until the formal request to leave Germany – usually a part of\nthe rejection of the asylum application – has been handed out to the person concerned and if sufficient\ngrounds for detention exist. However, it has been alleged that the authorities often order detention even\nif these conditions are not met (in the same manner as in other cases of detention pending removal, see\nAlternatives to detention). It can be assumed, based on the comparable low number of places which are\navailable in detention facilities, that most Dublin transfers take place within one day and therefore are\npreceded only by short-term arrests, in contrast to detention in a specialised facility which has to be\nordered by a judge (see also Detention).\n\n\n\n\n332\n      Taz.de, Nach Bruch des Kirchenasyls in Viersen: Doch keine Abschiebung, 25 July 2023, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/3OPmRxz.\n333\n      ZEIT Online, Demonstration gegen Bruch des Kirchenasyls in Schwerin, 28 December 2023, avaialble in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/48qRXTc.\n334\n      Ministry for Children, Youth, Family, Equality, Refugees and Integration of North Rhine Westphalia,\n      Kirchenasyl in Dublin-Fällen, 9 November 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/49Hg4xX.\n335\n      Section 1a (3) asylum Seekers Benefits Act.\n336\n      Infomigrants, Bremen court ruling: Benefits can be cut for migrants receiving church asylum, 13 Decemeber\n      2023, avaialbel in German at: https://bit.ly/4bJIOba.\n337\n      ECRE, The AnkER centres Implications for asylum procedures, reception and return, April 2019, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/2W7dICZ.\n338\n      Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 19/31669, 04 August 2021, available\n      in German at: https://bit.ly/4awfTGM, 117 et seq.\n\n\n                                                      62\n\n2.3    Personal interview\n\n                                   Indicators: Dublin: Personal Interview\n                                           Same as regular procedure\n\n      1. Is a personal interview of the asylum seeker in most cases conducted in practice in the Dublin\n         procedure?                                                                Yes     No\n         v If so, are interpreters available in practice, for interviews?          Yes     No\n\n      2. Are interviews conducted through video conferencing?            Yes     No     Information unavailable\n\nThere is no consistent practice regarding the timing of interviews in Dublin procedures. For the authorities\na Dublin procedure means that responsibilities are transferred to the ‘Dublin units’ of the BAMF, which\nmay happen at various stages of the procedure. In practice, the Dublin and regular procedure are carried\nout simultaneously. The personal hearing in the framework of the Dublin procedure is to be conducted, if\npossible, immediately after the registration of the asylum application, during which a first interview is\nconducted to establish the basic facts of a case in relation to the possible responsibility of another Member\nState to carry out the asylum procedure. In many cases, however, the personal interview is conducted a\nfew days after the registration, sometimes even later and when the BAMF has already received a reply\nfrom the Member State to which it has sent a take charge or take back request, but in any case before a\ndecision of inadmissibility is issued, unless the interview can be waived in accordance with Art. 5 (2) of\nthe Dublin regulation.339\nIn this Dublin interview, applicants should be given an opportunity to provide possible reasons why a\nremoval to another Dublin state could be impeded (e.g. existence of relatives in Germany). According to\nBAMF internal guidelines of December 2022, even if there are reasons to believe that another Member\nState might be responsible, the BAMF case officer is to conduct a personal interview related to the\ngrounds for asylum (see Regular procedure – Personal interview) after the ‘Dublin interview’ to increase\nefficiency of the procedure.340 In this context it has been noted that questions on the travel routes of\nasylum seekers may take up a considerable part of the interview, which, when both interviews are\nconducted on the same day, risk result in a shifting of focus away from the core issues of the personal\ninterview due to time constraints.\n\nWhereas before the outbreak of Covid-19, a face to face interview was mandatory for the admissibility\ninterview,341 the reform of the Asylum Act through Act on the acceleration of asylum court proceedings\nand asylum procedures342 which entered into force on 1 January 2023 introduced the possibility to conduct\nvideo interviews, including for Dublin interviews (see Personal interview).Even before, this possibility had\nbeen introduced for Dublin interviews as of July 2021.343 In 2023, 715 video interviews were conducted\n(see Regular procedure - Personal interview). It is not possible to say how many of these were purely\nrelated to admissibility according to the Dublin regulation however.\n\n\n\n\n339\n        Diakonie Deutschland, PRO ASYL & Informationsverbund Asyl & Migration, Das Dublin-Verfahren.\n        Grundlagen, Verfahrensablauf und Praxistipps, January 2024, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/42PClHq, 54.\n340\n        BAMF, Dienstanweisung Dublin (internal directive for Dublin procedures), version of December 2022,\n        available in German at: https://bit.ly/3J5jPTA.\n341\n        Entscheiderbrief, 9/2013, 3.\n342\n        Official Gazette I no. Nr. 56 (2022) of 28 December 2022, 2817.\n343\n        BAMF, Dienstanweisung Asyl (internal directive for asylum procedures), 03 August 2021, available in German\n        at: https://bit.ly/49mypAr, 104.\n\n\n                                                        63\n\n2.4    Appeal\n\n                                         Indicators: Dublin: Appeal\n                                           Same as regular procedure\n\n      1. Does the law provide for an appeal against the decision in the Dublin procedure?\n                                                                    Yes             No\n           v If yes, is it                                          Judicial        Administrative\n           v If yes, is it suspensive                               Yes             No\n\nDublin decisions are inadmissibility decisions under Section 29 of the Asylum Act.\n\nIt is possible to lodge an appeal against a Dublin decision before an Administrative Court within 1 week\nof notification. This appeal has no automatic suspensive effect; suspensive effect can be restored only\nupon request to the court. Once an application to restore suspensive effect has been filed, the transfer to\nanother Member State cannot take place until the court has decided on this request. The transfer can be\nexecuted only if the applicant misses the deadline or if the court rejects the application for suspensive\neffect. As of 1 January 2023, following the 2022 Act on the acceleration of asylum court proceedings and\nasylum procedures,344 courts have discretion on whether to hold personal hearings if the applicant is\nrepresented by a lawyer. However, a hearing must take place if the applicant requests so.345\n\nMaterial requirements for a successful appeal remain difficult to fulfil and the way these requirements\nmust be defined in detail remains a highly controversial issue. For example, administrative courts in the\nFederal States continue to render diverging decisions regarding whether problems in the different Member\nStates’ asylum systems amount to ‘systemic deficiencies’ or not (see Suspension of transfers).\n\nIn addition, serious practical difficulties result from the 7-day time limit for the necessary application to the\ncourt. This short deadline is often difficult to meet for asylum seekers since the parallel application for\nsuspensive effect must be fully substantiated. To prepare such an application requires expert knowledge\nof the asylum law, but in the absence of systematic legal counselling asylum seekers regularly have to\nturn to a lawyer or to refugee counsellors for assistance. However, it might prove impossible for asylum\nseekers to make an appointment with lawyers or counsellors within the short timeframe. Even if they\nmanage to contact a lawyer, it is still very difficult to produce a sufficiently substantiated application at\nsuch short notice. Therefore, it has been argued that the one-week period, although being an\nimprovement compared to the previous situation, still does not provide for an effective remedy and might\nconstitute a violation of the German Constitution.346\n\nIn May 2017, the Federal Constitutional Court established some general standards for the appeal\nprocedure in Dublin cases and cases of removals of people who have been granted protection status in\na third country. With regard to the case at hand, where the Administrative Court had rejected an application\nto restore suspensive effect of an appeal against a removal to Greece, the Court stated that the reception\nconditions in another country have to be assessed on a factual basis which is ‘reliable and sufficient, also\nconcerning the amount [of available information].’ This is necessary, in any case, if there were grounds to\nassume that inhuman or degrading treatment might take place following a removal. If sufficient information\non the factual situation in another country was not available, suspensive effect of the appeal should be\ngranted. In line with the general principle of judicial independence, the Constitutional Court did not define\nwhich kind of information was necessary to clarify the factual situation. It only pointed to the general\nobligation for authorities and courts to obtain information about conditions in other countries and to obtain\nindividual guarantees, if necessary.347\n\n\n344\n      Official Gazette I no. Nr. 56 (2022) of 28 December 2022, 2817.\n345\n      Section 77(2) Asylum Act.\n346\n      Dominik Bender and Maria Bethke, ‘‘Dublin III‘, Eilrechtsschutz und das Comeback der Drittstaatenregelung.’\n      Asylmagazin 11/2013, available in German at: https://bit.ly/4ar98Gl, 362.\n347\n      BverfG, Decision 2 BvR 157/17, 8 May 2017, asyl.net, available at: http://bit.ly/2G6rw9X.\n\n\n                                                       64\n\nThe following table illustrates the number of court decisions on requests for urgent legal protection i.e.\nrequests to restore suspensive effect of appeals in Dublin cases in 2023. standardised forms with\nwhich the Federal States can communicate detected vulnerabilities and specifics to the BAMF and vice\nversa.524 According to the BAMF, the procedures to identity vulnerabilities are laid down in its internal\nregulations, while the concept gives the BAMF staff comprehensive information on the detection and\ntreatment of vulnerable persons and is binding in so far as the internal guidelines refer to it.525\n\nAccording to the BAMF, the identification of vulnerable applicants as required by the APD is primarily the\nremit of the Federal States, who are responsible for reception and accommodation. Additionally, since\n2022 the BAMF internal guidelines lay down the standards for the BAMF employees to identify\nvulnerabilities in order to guarantee a fair asylum procedure for the persons concerned.526 These\nguidelines are updated regularly and are to be used conjointly with the concept mentioned above. A 2016\namendment to the German Asylum Act introduced wording relevant to the identification of vulnerable\nasylum seekers by allowing Federal States to transmit personal information about an applicant’s\nvulnerabilities to the BAMF. In turn, the BAMF has the obligation to transmit relevant information on\nvulnerabilities to the Federal States if they are necessary for adequate accommodation.527\nHowever, lacking clear duties of identification, the Asylum Act still fails to properly transpose the recast\nAPD, as it only requires the BAMF to ‘duly carry out’ the interview and not to provide ‘adequate support’\nto applicants in need of special procedural guarantees throughout the duration of the procedure.528 In\npractice, therefore, identification procedures in Germany have been described as ‘a matter of luck and\ncoincidence’, given that authorities ‘are not able to systematically undertake the necessary steps to\nascertain mental disorders or trauma.’529\n\nPrior to the revision of the law on counselling in 2023, the BAMF stated that the counselling service for\nasylum-seekers, consisting of general information on the procedure as well as the opportunity to make\nindividual appointments with BAMF staff (see Information for asylum seekers and access to NGOs and\nUNHCR) has led to vulnerabilities ‘being partially identified more often’ as counsellors inform applicants\nabout rights of vulnerable applicants during the procedure. As of 1 January 2023, the BAMF provides\nadditional funding for independent counsellors providing support for vulnerable groups.530 The\nindependent counsellors are required to transmit personal information to the BAMF and the Federal States\nagencies responsible for the accommodation of asylum seekers which is relevant for the identification\nand support of vulnerable groups, if the applicant consents.531 However, no details were given concerning\nthe number or the type of vulnerabilities which were identified in the course of the new advice service.\nAccording to information provided by the BAMF, no data are collected on vulnerabilities detected during\nthe counselling nor on the number of vulnerable persons applying for asylum in Germany.532 The BAMF\naffirms that the funding for independent counsellors is dependent on relevant certifications and\n\n\n\n523\n      BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Umsetzung in der Praxis des Bundesamtes\n      für Migration und Flüchtlinge, June 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Z7JHCU.\n524\n      The forms are available on the website of the Refugee Council North Rhine Westphalia at\n      http://bit.ly/3GMc5Do.\n525\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023.\n526\n      See BAMF, Dienstanweisung Asyl (internal directive for asylum procedures), – 2. Identifizierung vulnerabler\n      Personen, version of January 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3J5jPTA, 288. The duty is based on\n      Section 24(1) Asylum Act, which obliges the BAMF to investigate the relevant facts in each asylum case.\n527\n      BAMF, Dienstanweisung Asyl (internal directive for asylum procedures) – 2.1 Verpflichtung zur Identifizierung\n      vulnerabler Personen, 2021, 81 at: https://bit.ly/48Gv3In.\n528\n      See Nina Hager and Jenny Baron, ‘Eine Frage von Glück und Zufall. Zu den Verfahrensgarantien für psychisch\n      Kranke oder Traumatisierte im Asylverfahren’ in Informationsverbund Asyl und Migration (ed), Beratung und\n      Rechtsschutz im Asylverfahren: Beilage zum Asylmagazin 7-8/2017, July 2017, available in German at: , 17-\n      26.\n529\n       For a recent example of criticism of the lack of vulnerability identification and specific assistance, see Refugee\n      Council Berlin, 13 March 2023, ‚ Kein Ort für Schutzsuchende: Notunterkunft im Flughafen Tegel schließen ‘,\n      available in German here.\n530\n      BAMF, Antrag auf Gewährung von Bundeszuwendung für die Durchführung der Rechtsberatung für queere\n      und weitere vulnerable Schutzsuchende, January 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/47SfOuC.\n531\n      Section 12a Asylum Act.\n532\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023.\n\n\n                                                          92\n\nqualifications for the identification of vulnerabilities, which should guarantee the effectiveness of the\nidentification of vulnerabilities.533\n\nPrior to the revision of the identification concept in 2022, the lack of a systematic identification processes\nfor vulnerable applicants had been subject to recurring criticism from NGOs534 and international\norganisations,535 and described as especially problematic in the context of the airport procedure by NGOs\n(see Border procedure (border and transit zones)). In 2023, the Federal working group on psycho-social\ncentres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs acknowledged that there\nhave been attempts mainly by the Federal States to address these shortcomings. However, the working\ngroup repeated the criticism of no systematic approach.536 Along with these policy demands, the\nassociations introduced a toolbox on vulnerabilities which provides guidance for counselling and the\nidentification of vulnerabilities.537\n\nThe procedures and practice of identification in reception centres, which are run by the Federal States,\nvary. Upon initial registration, all asylum seekers should undergo a medical examination, which usually\ntakes place shortly after the registration of the asylum application in the arrival centre. However, this\nexamination is focused on the detection of communicable diseases and does not include a screening for\npotential vulnerabilities. Sometimes medical personnel or other staff members working in the reception\ncentres inform the BAMF if they recognise symptoms of trauma, the BAMF provides notification sheets\nwith which vulnerabilities can be communicated but there is no systematic procedure in place ensuring\nthat such information is passed on.538\n\nAs of 2020, only three Federal States (Berlin, Brandenburg and Lower Saxony) had a structured\nprocedure in place to identify particularly vulnerable asylum seekers.539 A number of States conduct\nscreenings, offer psychiatric or psychological consultations or refer to the general care infrastructure, and\nsome Federal States have integrated identification in their concepts for protection from violence in\nreception centres (Hamburg, Hesse, North Rhine Westphalia, Rhineland-Palatinate, Saarland,\nSaxony, Schleswig-Holstein).540 Since 2021, a project led by the Federal working group on psycho-\nsocial centres for refugees and victims of torture in cooperation with several NGOs as well as the BAMF\nand local authorities has developed a concept to identify vulnerable applicants in reception centres and\nin psycho-social centres. The concept was piloted in two reception centres in North Rhine Westphalia and\nBremen and the findings were published in March 2023 in a toolbox of guidelines which provide guidance\nfor counselling and the identification of vulnerabilities.541 The working group continues its work, and the\nFederal government affirms that it supports those Federal States who are interested in implementing the\n\n\n\n\n533\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC.\n534\n      KOK, Betroffene von Menschenhandel im Asylkontext erkennen. Problembeschreibung und\n      Handlungsempfehlungen, 2020, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3RuJcAY, 4.\n535\n      GRETA, Report concerning the implementation of the Council of Europe Convention on Action against\n      Trafficking in Human Beings by Germany, 20 June 2019, available at: https://bit.ly/3FKhZ8k, 30.\n536\n      BAfF, Policy Paper & Toolbox besondere Schutzbedarfe, 27 March 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UbSzIU.\n537\n      BAfF, Policy Paper & Toolbox besondere Schutzbedarfe, 27 March 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UbSzIU.\n538\n      Flüchtlingsrat Baden-Württemberg, Arbeitshilfe „Unterstützung geflüchteter Frauen“, 19 April 2022, available\n      in German at: https://bit.ly/3K7uMo8, 2.\n539\n      BAfF, Identifizierung besonderer Schutzbedürftigkeit am Beispiel von Personen mit Traumafolgestörungen,\n      30 June 2020, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3UdHuqH, 24f.\n540\n      BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Umsetzung in der Praxis des Bundesamtes\n      für Migration und Flüchtlinge, June 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Z7JHCU, 16.\n541\n      BAfF, Policy Paper & Toolbox besondere Schutzbedarfe, 27 March 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UbSzIU.\n\n\n                                                       93\n\nguidelines. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin.
