If the questionnaire indicates a potential vulnerability,\na screening interview takes place with the socio-psychological service of the Brandenburg immigration\nauthority (Zentrale Ausländerbehörde). Following the screening interview, if a vulnerability is detected\napplicants are referred to psychiatric counselling (which only takes place in Eisenhüttenstadt) and can be\nhoused in a special house for vulnerable applicants. The vulnerability is also communicated to the BAMF.\nHowever, in 2016 this was the case for under 1% of all asylum seekers, indicating that detection rates are\nvery low compared to the estimated prevalence of psychological distress among asylum seekers.\nFurthermore, the special accommodation houses both single men with psychological difficulties and single\nwomen who might have been victims of sexual violence.553 The Brandenburg Refugee Council criticises\nthat while there is a coordinated approach to identify vulnerabilities, the support measures vary depending\non the local governance. Accordingly, in some regions no specialised services for the accommodation of\nasylum seekers with special needs are available.554\n\nIn Lower Saxony, projects to improve the identification of vulnerable groups have been established in\nreception centres first in Friedland in 2012 and have since then expanded to all reception centres in the\nFederal State.555 Upon registration, all applicants are informed about special vulnerabilities during a\nmeeting with the social service of the reception centre and a further diagnosis is carried out in cases\nwhere there are indications of psychological disorders. While the authorities usually follow the\nrecommendations which follow from the diagnosis, between 2015 and 2017 only very few people were\nreferred for a diagnosis.556\n\nIn e.g., Rhineland-Palatinate,557 the regional government has adopted a protection concept which also\nincludes methods for the identification of vulnerabilities.558 This includes the following measures:\n     v Obligation to check for possible vulnerabilities in the reception centres during the initial stages of\n         the reception process and the asylum procedure;\n     v Intensification of communication between various actors and authorities involved in the reception\n         system and in the first steps of the asylum procedure;\n     v Documentation of possible vulnerabilities in a data system used by all authorities involved in the\n         reception process and in the asylum procedure;\n     v Training measures for persons employed by the Federal State in the reception centres to raise\n         awareness on the different forms of vulnerabilities.\n\n551\n      Berlin Senate, Senat beschließt Verfahren zur Unterstützung für besonders schutzbedürftige Geflüchtete aus\n      der Ukraine, 5 April 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48NlJCt.\n552\n      Refugee Council Berlin, Kein Ort für Schutzsuchende: Notunterkunft im Flughafen Tegel schließen, 13 March\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Ufa7Um.\n553\n      See BafF, ‘Identifizierung besonderer Schutzbedürftigkeit am Beispiel von Personen mit\n      Traumafolgestörungen. their roles covered unaccompanied children, gender-specific persecution, trauma or torture, and trafficking, with 410 officers qualified for unaccompanied-children cases.", "question_ids": ["3B.2", "3B.7", "4B.5", "6B.4", "8B.4"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly concerns staffing and institutional capacity in June 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "BAMF recruited, trained, and deployed the special officers under the federal government during Scholz's chancellorship.", "limitations": ["Some officers held multiple specialisations, so the category totals cannot be summed.", "The source does not show whether staffing was sufficient for workload or whether outcomes improved nationwide."], "start_unit": 98, "end_unit": 99, "locator": "page 98 through page 99", "exact_excerpt": "Moreover, young persons usually lose any entitlement to be supported in legal matters by the youth\nauthorities once they are declared to be adults in the course of the age assessment.574\n\nLatest numbers from 2021 show that 3,999 unaccompanied minors have been taken into custody. For\n1,608 of those this measure was ended due to an age assessment.575\n\n      2. Special procedural guarantees\n\n                                    Indicators: Special Procedural Guarantees\n\n      1. Are there special procedural arrangements/guarantees for vulnerable people?\n                                                           Yes        For certain categories      No\n             v If for certain categories, specify which: Unaccompanied children, traumatised persons\n                                                         Victims of torture or violence\n\n         2.1. Adequate support during the interview\n\nThe BAMF does not have specialised units dealing with vulnerable groups. According to the BAMF, all\ncase workers complete the EUAA training module on ‘Interviewing Vulnerable Persons’.576 If an applicant\nor a Federal State authority submits information to the BAMF that indicates vulnerability (such as medical\nrecords or information about specific physical, mental, intellectual or sensory impairments), such\ninformation is transferred to the case worker in charge who can decide to take measures such as allocate\nmore time for the interview, appoint an interpreter of a specific gender or allow the person to bring a\ntrusted person of their choice to the interview.577\n\nFor specific groups of vulnerable persons, the BAMF employs ‘special officers’ (Sonderbeauftragte)\nresponsible for interviews and decisions on claims by applicants with special needs. Special officers also\nadvise their colleagues in dealing with vulnerable applicants and are contact persons for specialised\ncounselling services and psycho-social centres.578 Staff members who become special officers must\ncomplete a training module for the specialisation they want to achieve. In addition, they follow the EASO\ntraining modules for their specialisation.579 Training covers both the identification and in the treatment of\nvulnerable persons.580 According to the BAMF, continuous training is offered for specific topics in the realm\nof the special officers’ responsibilities.581 The BAMF guidelines stipulate that the following cases shall be\nhandled in a particularly sensitive manner and, if necessary, by specially-trained decision-makers:582\n     v Unaccompanied children;\n     v Victims of gender-specific prosecution;\n     v Victims of human trafficking; and\n     v Victims of torture and traumatised asylum seekers.\n\nAs of June 2023, a total of 1,267 BAMF had one or more roles as special officers. This corresponds to\nroughly a third of full-time equivalent positions allocated to the first instance procedure (see Number of\n\n574\n        Nerea González Méndez de Vigo, Alterseinschätzung – ein Irrgarten ohne Ausweg? The distribution among areas of responsibilities was the\nfollowing: Unaccompanied children (410), victims of gender-specific persecution (312) traumatised\npersons and victims of torture (291), victims of trafficking (254).583\n\nSpecially trained case officers may be included at all times of the asylum procedure, or take over, also\nprior to the interview, if vulnerabilitites are known. For example, if it becomes evident during the interview\nthat an asylum seeker belongs to one of these groups, the officer conducting the interview is obliged to\nconsult a special officer, in addition to notifying the reception centre if necessary and authorised by the\napplicant.584 A note on how the officers are planning to proceed must be added by the special officer to\nthe file, particularly if the special officer takes over the case as a result of their consultation. According to\ninformation provided by the government, there is an obligation in cases of unaccompanied minors for\nspecial officers to take over responsibility for the asylum procedures. In other cases of other vulnerable\ngroups, the specially trained case officer must be consulted and there are two options for further\nprocedures: either the special officer adopts an advisory role or they take over responsibility for the\nprocedure.585 However, the BAMF does not record the number of cases in which special officers are\nconsulted or in which procedures are handed over to special officers.586\n\nLawyers have reported that the introduction of special officers has led to some improvement in the\nhandling of ‘sensitive’ cases. The special officers receive special training in the area of LGBTIQ, they\nconduct interviews upon consultation by other BAMF officers and serve as multipliers for their\ncolleagues.587However, there have also been examples of cases in which indications of trauma and even\nexplicit references to torture did not lead to special officers being consulted.588 It has further been criticised\nthat there are shorcomings in the effective implementation of procedural guarantees for LGBTIQ+\npersons, which increases the risk of false decisions.589 There is no individual right to have a special officer\nhandling a person’s case, except for unaccompanied minors for whom this is mandatory. But if evidence\nsuggests that the person is vulnerable or if the person claims to have certain vulnerabilities, the interviewer\nis required to involve a special officer in the procedure, e.g. as consultant.590 However, the Administrative\nCourt of Berlin ruled that if special vulnerabilities have been detected, the absence of a special officer in\nthe asylum procedure constitutes a violation of procedural rights of vulnerable asylum seekers.591\n\n        2.2. Exemption from special procedures\n\nThe German Asylum Act exempts neither unaccompanied children nor persons with special procedural\nguarantees from the airport procedure, despite an express obligation under the recast APD to provide for\nsuch exemptions under certain conditions.592 It also makes no reference to ‘adequate support’ which\nshould be provided to those requiring special procedural guarantees.593\n\n\n\n\n583\n       The government notes that the figures cannot be added since some officers may have qualified in more than\n       one area; Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/7503, 28 June 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sb12cF, 2-4.\n584\n       BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. In particular, the former\nprovision could only be applied if return to the safe ‘other third country’ was possible within 3 months.\nAlthough this qualification has been removed, the provision has been applied rarely, only 24 times in 2020,\n4 times in 2021, 6 times in 2022 and 3 times in the first half of 2023 (see Admissibility procedure).692\n\n\n G. Information for asylum seekers and access to NGOs and UNHCR\n\n      1. Provision of information on the procedure\n\n                                 Indicators: Information on the Procedure\n      1. Is sufficient information provided to asylum seekers on the procedures, their rights and obligations\n         in practice?                  Yes             With difficulty         No\n\n              v Is tailored information provided to unaccompanied children?                    Yes      No\n\nAccording to Section 24(1) of the Asylum Act, the BAMF:\n\n          ‘... [S]hall inform the foreigner early on in a language he can reasonably be supposed to\n          understand about the course of the procedure and about his rights and duties, especially\n          concerning deadlines and the consequences of missing a deadline, and about possibilities to\n          return voluntarily.’\n\nThe provision was changed with the entry into force of the 2022 Act on the acceleration of asylum court\nproceedings and asylum procedures on 1 January 2023.693 The reform introduced the requirement of\ninforming applicants “early on” instead of “after the lodging of the asylum application”, which was the\nprevious wording. Information is to be provided orally in groups (see\n\nOral information). Another change introduced by the reform is the duty to inform not only about the\nasylum procedure, but also about possibilities to return voluntarily after the rejection of the asylum\napplication.\n\nFor the impact of the Covid-19 outbreak on information provision to asylum applicants see the 2021\nUpdate to the AIDA Country Report for Germany.694\n\n          1.1. Written information\n\nVarious other sections of the Asylum Act also contain obligations on the authorities to inform asylum\nseekers on certain aspects of the procedure. Accordingly, asylum seekers receive various information\nsheets when reporting to the authorities and/or upon arrival at the initial reception centre,695 including the\nfollowing:\n     v An information sheet on the rights and duties during the procedure and on the proceedings in\n        general (‘Belehrung nach § 10 AsylG und allgemeine Verfahrenshinweise’), to be handed out by\n        the authority where an applicant first voices the wish to apply for asylum (the border police, the\n\n\n\n\n692\n        Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/432, 14 January 2022, available in\n        German at: https://bit.ly/3RvW8GL, 6; 19/18498, 2 April 2020, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/3RPHZFG, 6. 20/8222, 5 September 2023, available at: https://bit.ly/3SklJCR. These instructions are available in 44 languages.\nThe applicant has to sign an acknowledgment of the receipt of the information leaflets. In some reception\ncentres, further information is handed out or made available through notice boards or posters (e.g.\ninformation on the office hours of authorities, NGOs and other institutions), but there is no systematic\npractice for the distribution of such additional information.\n\nIt has been a long-standing criticism from lawyers and NGOs that both the written instructions and the\noral briefings provided by the Federal Office are ‘rather abstract and standardised’.706 In 2016 it was\nparticularly criticised, that the information is notsuitable to render the significance and content of questions\nduring interviews sufficiently understandable to applicants. In the ‘Memorandum to enhance fair and\ndiligent asylum procedures in Germany’, published by an alliance of 12 German NGOs in November 2016,\n\n\n\n696\n        DA-AVS (internal directives for the asylum procedure secretariat), 80, version as of March 2014, available in\n        German at: https://bit.ly/3QPQZsl, 80.\n697\n        Available on the BAMF website at: https://bit.ly/2U0lyyv.\n698\n        Available on the BAMF website at: http://bit.ly/3XGnpYs.\n699\n        Available on the BAMF website at: http://bit.ly/3IWpqM0.\n700\n        As of January, these were Albanian, Amharic, Arabic. Memorandum zur derzeitigen Situation des deutschen Asylverfahrens\n        (Memoranda on current situation of the German asylum procedure), 2005, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/4buofPY, 21.\n\n\n                                                         114\n\nseveral deficiencies were identified in the context of the right to information.707 Since autumn 2015, the\nBAMF has developed a number of new, more accessible information products, including information on\nthe website, leaflets, explainer videos and an app for newly arrived refugees.708 Nevertheless,\nstakeholders reported that especially for asylum seekers with disabilities, such concerns persist to date.709\n\n        1.2. Oral information\n\nOral information for asylum applicants now mainly consists of the ‘voluntary independent state-run\ncounselling’ that was introduced with the so-called ‘Orderly-Return-Law’, in force since 21 August 2019\n(Section 12a Asylum Act). With the entry into force of the 2022 Act on the acceleration of asylum court\nproceedings and asylum procedures710 on 1 January 2023, the state-run counselling is to be replaced by\nindependent counselling, financed by the Federal Government but carried out by welfare associations or\n‘other civil society actors’.711 This is in line with long-standing demands form welfare associations (see\nbelow). Counselling consists of two stages: group sessions with basic information on the asylum\nprocedure as well as on return procedures, followed by the second stage of individual counselling\nsessions. The BAMF will continue to carry out the first stage of counselling as described below, whereas\nindependent organisations will carry out individual counselling.712 The funding process for independent\ncounselling associations started in February 2023 where associations could file interest for funding. After\na summary oversight, the BAMF then required the associations to file the encompassing application for\nfunding.713 EUR 20 million of financing were provided for in 2023. Welfare organisations criticise that the\nmoney was only disbursed in the summer of 2023, which delayed the availability of independent\ncounselling or caused financial gaps for those associations which provided counselling services prior to\nthe official distribution of funding. Additionally, NGOs have been criticial of the amount foreseen, stating\nthat EUR 20 million is not sufficient for nationwide independent counselling. From the AnkER centre in\nManching-Ingoldstadt, the NGO in charge can currently offer two fulltime counselling positions for up to\n600 asylum applicants, even though the BAMF’s general recommendation is one fulltime position for 180\nasylum applicants.714 The sum to be spent for personnel suffices only for early career and not for\nexperienced personnel, which make it difficult to find employees and which in combination with the high\nnumber of cases, causes an overburdening of the staff.715 Despite the envisaged goal of EUR 80 million\nannually, for 2024 again only EUR 25 million are calculated, this time for the whole year, not as in 2023\nfor the second half of the year.716 According to welfare associations, the insecurity as to how much funding\nwill be provided in the upcoming years and under which circumstances the funding will be awarded has\nled to the withdrawal of associations from their funding applications for the counselling service.717\n\n\n\n\n707\n      Memorandum Alliance, Memorandum für faire und sorgfältige Asylverfahren in Deutschland. Since there are no federal rules governing the access, it is up to the discretion of the local\nauthorities whether welfare associations have access to the centres. In Munich, the Refugee Council\ntried to provide independent mobile counselling prior 2023 and has been denied access. The Federal\nAdministrative Court upheld the denial in 2023. The court decided that access must be granted in\nindividual case after registration and only where an asylum applicant has demanded counselling.\nHowever, local authorities are not obliged to grant open access to the facilities.718 This leads to legal\nuncertainty as to whether systematic access will be provided to welfare associations under the new rules\non counselling. Overall, several associations criticise that due to the lack of funding, the uncoordinated\nfunding process and the legal uncertainty as to whether access to accommodations centres is provided,\naccess to individual counselling cannot be guaranteed in Germany.\n\nPrior to the reforms in January 2023, government advice covered the period from the lodging of the asylum\napplication to the explanation of a first instance decision; now the legal conselling can also cover appeal\nproceedings.719 According to the BAMF, the staff who offered the counselling underwent a one-week\ntraining and was ‘organisationally separated from the asylum area’.720 Procedure counselling was first\nintroduced in a pilot project together with welfare associations.721 It was then established first in all AnkER\nand functionally equivalent centres and has been rolled out to the rest of the BAMF branch offices since\n2020.722\n\nAs of 31 December 2022, counselling was available in 46 BAMF branch offices.723 Throughout 2022,\n37,644 applicants took part in the first stage counselling, while 3,147 received individual counselling\n(second stage). This is an increase in comparison to 2021 (1,928 individual sessions, while 25,784\npersons took part in group sessions), but still shows that only around 15% of the 244,132 persons who\napplied for asylum in 2022 (see Statistics) received individual advice. More information on counselling\nduring the Covid-19 outbreak can be found in the 2021 Update to the AIDA Country Report for Germany.724\nNo information on the availability of counselling and on the number of sessions is available for 2023 as of\nApril 2024.\n\nThe BAMF counselling sessions represent an improvement compared to the situation prior to August 2019\nwhen no information was systematically provided to asylum seekers.725 Nevertheless, the system is\nheavily criticised by NGOs as group counselling sessions tend to be organised within a very short period\nbefore the personal interview with the BAMF and the information provided is limited (i.e. the BAMF tends\nto provide general information on the asylum procedure, sometimes focusing only on asylum seekers’\nobligations and also on information which has nothing to do with the procedure, such as the so-called\n‘return options’).726\n\nIn addition to the counselling services as regulated by the asylum act, asylum seekers are orally informed\nabout ‘the significance and the proceedings of the interview’ and they are instructed about their rights and\n\n\n718\n      Federal Administrative Court, Decision 1 C 40.21, 28 March 2023, available in Germant at:\n      https://bit.ly/480lN0o, para. 27f.\n719\n      Section 12a (2) German Asylum Act.\n720\n      See AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2022, April 2023, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UChWUr, 99.\n721\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023. Asylum seekers reportedly undergo the airport procedure without understanding the\napplicable rules and steps729 (see also Border procedure (border and transit zones)). The welfare\nassociation Caritas hopes that the funding for independent counselling will also enhance the availability\nof counselling services at the airport but asserts for 2023 that there is not enough available data yet to\nevaluate whether there have been any improvements.730\n\n      2. Access to NGOs and UNHCR\n\n                              Indicators: Access to NGOs and UNHCR\n      1. Do asylum seekers located at the border have effective access to NGOs and UNHCR if they wish\n         so in practice?           Yes              With difficulty         No\n\n      2. Do asylum seekers in detention centres have effective access to NGOs and UNHCR if they wish\n         so in practice?           Yes             With difficulty         No\n\n      3. Do asylum seekers accommodated in remote locations on the territory (excluding borders) have\n         effective access to NGOs and UNHCR if they wish so in practice?\n                                         Yes              With difficulty          No\n\nWelfare organisations and other NGOs offer free advice services which include basic legal advice.731\nHowever, access to NGOs is highly dependent on the place of residence. In some reception centres,\nwelfare organisations or refugee councils have regular office hours or are located close to the centres so\nasylum seekers can easily access the offices of such organisations. However, offices of NGOs do not\nexist in all relevant locations and in any case, access to such services is not systematically ensured. As\nof 2023, there is no mechanism at the federal level which ensures that asylum seekers are getting access\nto legal advice from an independent institution before the interview. In contrast, the Federal Administrative\nCourt decided in 2023 that there is no obligation to provide regular access to reception centres for welfare\nassociations. Only in cases where counselling was explicitly requested by the asylum applicant and the\nrespective welfare association received a mandate to counsel this individual applicant, access needs to\nbe granted.732 It is uncertain how the legal reforms of 2023 to the independent counselling will affect the\nimplementation of the court ruling (see: Provision of information on the procedure).\n\nIn AnkER centres in Bavaria, access of NGOs depends on the management of the centre. In the AnKER\nRegensburg for example, Caritas, Amnesty International, the Refugee law clinic Regensburg and\nCampus Asyl have access to the facility, while in Manching/Ingolstadt, only Caritas has established\npresence. In the experience of certain NGOs, asylum seekers are not systematically re-directed to NGOs\nfor further information. In centres such as Manching/Ingolstadt and Regensburg, NGOs have further\nno way of ensuring systematic counselling sessions with every new arrival, since they do not receive the\nregistration list of residents in the AnKER centre.733\n\n727\n        Section 24 (1) Asylum Act.\n728\n        BAMF, DA-Asyl (Dienstanweisung Asylverfahren) – Belehrungen (internal directives of the BAMF), version as\n        of 1 January 2023, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3J5jPTA.\n729\n        ECRE, Airport procedures in Germany Gaps in quality and compliance with guarantees, April 2019, available\n        at: https://bit.ly/2QgOmAH.\n730\n        Caritas, Auch im Schnellverfahren am Flughafen die Rechte wahren, 11 December 2023, available in German\n        at: https://bit.ly/49eEcHY.\n731\n        A database of advice services for asylum seekers is available at: https://bit.ly/2Ho73Az.\n732\n        Federal Administrative Court, Decision 1 C 40.21, 28 March 2023, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/480lN0o, para. 27f.\n733\n        ECRE, The AnkER centres Implications for asylum procedures, reception and return, April 2019, available at:\n        https://bit.ly/2W7dICZ.\n\n\n                                                        117\n\nIn other arrival or AnkER centres established since 2016, access to NGOs is made even more difficult as\nthese do not have offices in the town or region where the new centres are located. A positive example is\nthe arrival centre at Heidelberg where the Federal State of Baden-Württemberg has established an\nindependent ‘qualified social and procedural advisory service’ in cooperation with welfare\norganisations.734 Within this model, a social worker from an independent organisation functions as contact\nperson for 100 asylum seekers and is explicitly commissioned to offer advice on the asylum procedure\n(while in many other reception centres social workers are not necessarily independent and/or they often\nare neither qualified nor entitled to offer counselling services on the asylum procedure).735 Even here, in\nthe past it has proven difficult for the social workers to effectively prepare asylum seekers for the interview\nin the asylum procedure since they are often approached with other urgent matters such as social support,\nfamily reunification etc.736\n\nFurthermore, despite an attempt at a progressive approach in Heidelberg interviews have been scheduled\nat very short notice in the arrival centres, at a time when asylum seekers have to come to terms with other\nadministrative regulations and with their new surroundings in general. In this situation, it has proven\ndifficult to create an adequate setting for the preparation for the interview.737 In the light of these problems\nbeing described in the context of the ‘arrival centre’ at Heidelberg, it can be concluded that access to\nNGOs is even more limited or may be excluded in many other locations where no similar structures exist.\nThis is particularly the case for the possibilities to access NGOs before the interview, since fast-tracking\nof procedures is taking place at a growing number of ‘arrival centres’ and AnkER-centres.\n\nFollowing an initial period in a reception centre, asylum seekers are usually referred to accommodation\ncentres or apartments in other places of residence (see Types of accommodation). Some of these\naccommodation centres are located in remote areas without proper access by means of public transport.\nIf the place of residence is located far away from the next town, travel costs to get there may also pose a\nserious problem in practice, since these costs would only be covered by public funds in exceptional cases.\nAccordingly, access to NGOs can be severely restricted under such circumstances.\n\nThe so-called ‘geographical restriction’ or ‘residence obligation’ (Residenzpflicht) also poses a legal\nobstacle for many asylum seekers who wanted to contact an NGO or lawyer. Beyond the obligation to\nstay in initial reception centres, a general residence obligation is imposed for asylum seekers from safe\ncountries of origin for the whole duration of their procedures (see Freedom of movement).738 Therefore\nthe ‘residence obligation’ and the obligation to remain in a particular reception centre pose serious\nobstacles for access to NGOs and UNHCR in many cases.\n\nFor information on access to NGOs during the airport procedure, see Border procedure (border and transit\nzones).\n\n\n\n\n734\n      Friedrich-Ebert-Stiftung, Das Ankunftszentrum Heidelberg als ‚Pate‘ für Ankerzentren?, 8 August 2018,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/3HKoSqV.\n735\n      Ibid.\n736\n      Ibid.\n737\n      Johannes Moll, ‘Das verkürzte Asylverfahren im Ankunftszentrum Heidelberg. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "After the Afghan admission programme resumed, 99 security-screening interviews were conducted between 26 June and 21 July 2023 and no security concerns were identified; the government reported expanded capacity to conduct several hundred screenings per month by August.", "question_ids": ["3B.2", "3B.7", "6B.3", "8B.3", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly concerns programme safeguards and capacity in mid-2023.", "ruler_attribution": "Federal security agencies and the responsible ministries implemented the screening system under the Scholz government.", "limitations": ["The sample is small and early; absence of identified concerns does not establish long-term security effectiveness.", "The capacity figure is a government-reported estimate."], "start_unit": 123, "end_unit": 124, "locator": "page 123 through page 124", "exact_excerpt": "groups/communities.’771 The admission programme includes family members of those persons, which\nincludes spouses or same sex partners, minor children and other family members who can prove a\nrelation of dependency (beyond economic dependency) with the main person and find themselves in a\nsituation of concrete and lasting danger due to the work or vulnerability of the main person.772 The German\ngovernment appoints agencies (including civil society organisations) who can put forward names of\nsuitable persons, who must still be living in Afghanistan, via an IT application containing a questionnaire\nof a total of 41 pages.773 The names of these organisations are not made public by the government, but\naccording to a press report, PRO ASYL, Reporters without Borders, Mission Lifeline and Luftbrücke Kabul\nare taking part in the programme as of 20 December 2022.774\n\nThe Government then takes the admission decision based on selection criteria that include vulnerability\n(in line with the UNHCR catalogue of criteria), relation to Germany e. g. through language skills, family\nties, previous stays or work for German authorities or projects, level of personal exposure of the person\ne. g. through a visible / exposed position or public statements, and a special political interest on the side\nof Germany to admit a person.775 As with the previous admission programme, selected persons first\nreceive assistance to leave Afghanistan and enter a neighbouring country and are then issued a visa and\ntravel assistance by the German embassy in that country. Persons who enter Germany under the\nprogramme receive a residence permit for three years. The Federal State responsible for reception of the\npersons is to be determined according to the quota system for the distribution of asylum seekers (see\nRegistration of the asylum application), although family ties and other ‘criteria supporting integration’ are\nto be taken into account.776\n\nWhen announcing the programme, the Federal Government declared that ‘the new programme is now to\nbe implemented quickly’ and that it planned to approve around 1,000 requests per month, which is about\nthe amount of permissions granted in the months preceding the announcement. The programme is\nplanned to run until the end of the current government’s term in 2025.777 As of 30 June 2023, 229 persons\nhave been selected for admission.778 As of October 2023, only 13 of them had been admitted, due to the\npause of the admission procedure (see below).779 According to a press report, the NGOs Mission Lifeline\nand Luftbrücke Kabul alone have received around 32,000 requests as of early November 2022.780\n\nIn March 2023, the Federal Foreign office declared that all admission programs would be put to a halt for\nan indefinite time due to alleged abuse. The German newspaper Cicer and Bild published that, according\nto information they received, the admission programs had been used to bring radical Islamists to\n\n\n\n771\n      Federal Foreign Office, Joint press release by the Federal Foreign Office and the Federal Ministry of the\n      Interior and Community and Community on the federal admission programme for people from Afghanistan\n      who are at particular risk, 17 October 2022, available at: http://bit.ly/3J47ZJA.\n772\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n773\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n774\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n775\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n776\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, Anordnung des\n      Bundesministeriums des Innern und für Heimat gemäß § 23 Absatz 2, Absatz 3 i. V. m. § 24 Aufenthaltsgesetz\n      (AufenthG) zur Aufnahme von besonders gefährdeten afghanischen Staatsangehörigen aus Afghanistan, 19\n      December 2022, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3GW7jmJ.\n777\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n778\n      Federal Government, Plenary protocol 20/114, 5 July 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3SqBvvZ,\n      question 28.\n779\n      International Rescue Committee (IRC), Ein Jahr Bundesaufnahmeprogramm für Afghanistan: Gemeinsamer\n      Aufruf von 7 NGOs zur Zwischenbilanz und Umsetzung der Verpflichtungen, 16 October 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/486jadA.\n780\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n\n\n                                                     123\n\nGermany.781 In April 2023, the Federal government rebutted these allegations. A speaker of the\nForeigner’s Office declared that there were no evidence supporting a systemic misuse of the admission\nprograms.782 Only in one case a person who applied for admission has been identified as a possible\n‘threat’ (‘Gefährder’). Nevertheless, the Foreigners Office along with the Minister of Interior decided to\nintroduce additional screening mechanisms and to halt the admission program until the screening\nmechanism is in place.783 The screening procedure involves an automatic data reconciliation with\ndatabases from the national security agencies and interviews with the German domestic intelligence\nservice (Bundesamt für Verfassungsschutz), Federal Criminal Police Office (Bundeskriminalamt), the\nFederal Police or BAMF officers acting on behalf of these national security services.784 The admission\nprograms were restarted from 26 June 2023. In the first months of the newly introduced screening\nprocedure (26 June – 21 July 2023) 99 screening interviews took place, no security concerns have been\nidentified among those.785 The Federal government further states, that the capacities for the screening\nmechanism have been expanded since its start. Accordingly, as of August 2023 several hundreds of\nscreenings can be conducted per month.786\n\nThe Left party and NGOs such as PRO ASYL welcomed the launch of the programme but criticised that\n1,000 admissions per month was too low given the ‘real pressure of persecution’ for ‘people who have\nfought for democracy and human rights’. PRO ASYL further criticised that the relatively abstract selection\ncriteria could lead to an ‘ethically highly ambivalent protection lottery’787 especially in connection with the\nfact that only authorised agencies could put forward people and that the application is through an\nalgorithm-based IT application with little room to put forward specific individual circumstances.788 The\nNGO Kabul Luftbrücke reported problems with the IT application in October and November, leading to\ndelays in sending the online forms to authorities. A further point of critique is that the programme does not\nextend to persons who have managed to flee Afghanistan.789 Several NGOs also voiced concerns over\nthe practical implementation, demanding a better staffing of the counselling and coordination centres for\nthe programme and questioning the ‘organization and content’ of the procedure,790 especially given that\nit is required to have a passport in order to leave the country while obtaining one is made extremely\ndifficult by the Taliban government. One year after the official launch, in October 2023, several NGOs\nreflected on the development of the program. They demanded that the originally envisaged number of\n1,000 admissions per months should be fulfilled, that the procedure should be more transparent and that\nin additional admission schemes are necessary to meet the needs for protection.791\n\nIn addition to the Federal Government, several Federal States (Thuringia, Berlin, Hessen and Bremen)\nhave implemented admission programmes based on family ties to Afghans living in the respective Federal\nStates (for more information see Family Reunification). Palestinian territories\n\nThe attack by the Hamas on Israel on the 7 October 2023 and the following escalating conflict has led to\npolitical discussions and rifts in the public perception. Following the attack, chancellor Scholz declared\nthe security of Israel as a reason of state for Germany. He claimed that Germany’s place is on the side of\nIsrael and that Germany stands in full solidarity and supports Israel.828 The German government continues\nto position itself in favour for Israel, e.g., by its abstention to the UN resolution and its rejection of an EU\nresolution on ceasefire. Only very wary and situational criticism to the Israelian government and its\nreaction to the attack by the German government have been voiced by the German government. At a\npress conference of the Foreigners Office on 9 October 2023, the speaker of the Foreigners Office\nmentioned that in the past the German government has voiced criticism to the Israelian handling of the\nmiddle-east conflict and generally envisages a two-state solution but that at the current moment the focus\nshould be the support of Israel’s defence.829 Since then, the Minister of Foreign Affairs increasingly raises\ncriticism to specific actions of the Israelian government, e.g., calling for humanitarian corridors and\nsupport, demanding the protection of civil society and the adherence to International Humanitarian Law.830\nNevertheless, the German government has announced to support Israel in the case pending before the\nInternational Criminal Court.831 The public perception is rifted. no personal Scholz action is shown.", "limitations": ["The accommodation-centre figure runs only through October 2023 and the source does not establish causation or government responsibility for the attacks."], "start_unit": 154, "end_unit": 155, "locator": "page 154 through page 155", "exact_excerpt": "Situation in collective accommodation centres and decentralised housing\n\nFollowing the initial reception period, asylum seekers are supposed to be sent to a collective\naccommodation centre within the same Federal State. However, responsibility for housing at this stage\nof the procedure often lies with the municipalities and many different forms of accommodation have been\nestablished. On the local level, accommodation may still consist of collective housing in former army\nbarracks, in (formerly empty) apartment blocks or in housing containers. At the same time, many\nmunicipalities have dissolved collective accommodation centres from the 1990s onwards and are now\npermitting asylum seekers to rent an apartment on the housing market or in council housing. As mentioned\nin Types of accommodation, decentralised accommodation is more common in some regions than in\nothers, so whether asylum seekers are housed in collective accommodation or in apartments depends\nheavily on the situation of the municipalities.\n\nStudies have repeatedly shown that living conditions of asylum seekers differ considerably between\nregions and sometimes even within the same town.1005 For example, some municipalities have a policy\nof generally allowing asylum seekers to live in apartments, which they have to find and rent on their own.\nIn some areas, this is almost impossible in practice for many asylum seekers, since rents are unaffordable\nin privately owned apartments and space in council housing is extremely limited. This may lead to a\nsituation in which asylum seekers have to stay in collective accommodation centres although they are\ntechnically not required to do so.\nBecause different policies are pursued on regional and local level, it is impossible to make general\nstatements on the standards of living in the follow-up accommodation facilities.\n\nIt has also been pointed out that living conditions in individual apartments are not necessarily better than\nin accommodation centres (e.g. if apartments are provided in run-down buildings or if decentralised\naccommodation is only available in isolated locations).1006 Nevertheless, the collective accommodation\ncentres, and particularly the bigger ones (often referred to as ‘camps’ by critics) are most often criticised\nby refugee organisations and other NGOs.1007\nFacilities are often isolated or in remote locations. Many temporary facilities do not comply with basic\nstandards and do not guarantee privacy.1008 According to reports this has led to serious health problems\nfor some asylum seekers, especially in cases of long stays in collective accommodation centres. In\nfacilities in which food is provided, asylum seekers are sometimes not allowed to prepare their own food\nand/or no cooking facilities exist. The quality of food is often criticised where food is handed out in the\nform of pre-packed meals.1009 In Lower-Saxony for example, one protection seeker reports that the food\nwas insufficient and inadequate especially for his special needs due to his cancer disease.1010 In one\naccommodation centre in Rhineland-Palatinate it has further been reported that the lightweight\nconstruction of the accommodation centre alongside with the asphalted surrounding without any shadow\nin summer the accommodation centre becomes nearly uninhabitably warm.1011\nConcerns have also been raised around limited space and equipment for recreation, including for children,\nin some facilities. In some centres, no separate and quiet space is available for children, for example to\ndo their homework for school.1012\n\n\n1005\n       Boris Kühn / Julian Schlicht, Mediendienst Integration, Kommunale Unterbringung von Geflüchteten –\n       Probleme und Lösungsansätze, July 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3SA0eiO.\n1006\n       BAMF, Die Wohnsituation Geflüchteter, February 2018, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3HxQH6B.\n1007\n       ProAsyl, ‘Ein Leben ohne Privatsphäre? Already in 2018 the\nGerman Institute for Human Rights published an analysis of common house rules in accommodation\nfacilities and plead that the right to privacy under Art. 13 (1) of the German Constitution applies to\ncollective accommodations and that therefore security personnel cannot unreasonably enter the private\nrooms.1013 In 2022 the Higher Administrative Court Baden-Wuerttemberg agreed with the position. It\ndecided that indeed private rooms in collective accommodations are protected under Art. 13 (1) of the\nGerman Constitution checks of private rooms therefore need to be regulated by law and justified, which\nis not the case if house rules generally allow for security personnel to enter private rooms.1014 In June\n2023, however, the Federal Administrative Court restricted this protection again.1015 The Court considered\nthe broad access and control rights of the security staff to be permissible under fundamental rights.\nHowever, these restrictions would have to be regulated by law.\n\nFurthermore, many facilities lack qualified staff, thus highlighting the crucial role played by NGOs and\nvolunteers, particularly regarding counselling and integration. However, there is currently a lack of\nvolunteers, which on top often feel left alone with their work.1016\n\n        2.2    Physical security\n\nIn addition to overall living conditions, the security of residents can also be an issue of concern. According\nto preliminary police statistics up to October 2023 30 attacks on accommodation centres were reported,\ncompared to 65 in 2022, 61 in 2021, 84 in 2020 and 128 in 2019. In addition, 167 attacks on individual\nasylum seekers or refugees were recorded in 2021 (1,606 in 2020). Most of these attacks are classified\nas racially motivated crimes.1017\nAn increase of attacks in general is still visible.1018 Although the number of attacks on accommodation\ncentres has lowered, the attacks on migrants, asylum seekers and refugees has risen from 1,371 in 2022\nto 1,515 in 2023.1019\n\nIn many facilities, spatial confinement and lack of privacy led to a lack of security, particularly for women\nand children.1020 To counter this problem, most Federal States have developed violence protection\nconcepts in recent years.1021 Additionally, the Federal Ministry for Family, Seniors, Women and Youth\nintroduced in 2019 a monitoring and evaluating programme which serves to develop common standards\nfor violence protection concepts.1022 Despite the introduced violence protection concepts, protection\nseekers continue to report violent and/or racial harassment from security personnel. Refugee Councils\nfrom several Federal states therefore call for a more effective implementation of the protection\n\n\n\n\n1013\n       Deutsches Institut für Menschenrechte, Hausordnungen menschenrechtskonform gestalten, 4 November\n       2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3JfFN6O.\n1014\n       VGH Baden-Wuerttemberg, Decision 12 S 4089/20, 2 February 2022, available at: https://bit.ly/3HvQJf1.\n1015\n       Federal Administrative Court, Judgment no. 1 C 10.22, 15 June 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/42OG34j.\n1016\n       Br.de, Flüchtlingshelfer fühlen sich von Politik alleingelassen, 05 March 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/4bHSR0n.\n1017\n       Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary questions by The Left, 20/49293, 14 November 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/49hgI5q; tagesschau.de, Wieder mehr Anschläge auf Flüchtlingsunterkünfte, 8\n       November 2022, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3iXRhBb.\n1018\n       Reliefweb.int, Xenophobic backlash: Germany must tackle alarming increase in assaults on asylum seekers,\n       available at: https://bit.ly/48gGzsX.\n1019\n       Infomigrants.net, Germany: Increase in attacks on migrants and asylum seekers, available at:\n       https://bit.ly/3I1a734.\n1020\n       ProAsyl, ‘Ein Leben ohne Privatsphäre? Legal framework of detention\n\n       1. Grounds for detention\n\n                                     Indicators: Grounds for Detention\n       1. In practice, are most asylum seekers detained\n              v on the territory:                                                  Yes              No\n              v at the border:                                                     Yes              No\n\n       2. Are asylum seekers detained in practice during the Dublin procedure?\n                                                             Frequently      Rarely                 Never\n\n       3. Are asylum seekers detained during a regular procedure in practice?\n                                                            Frequently       Rarely                 Never\n\nLegal changes through the Act on the Improvement of Removals\n\nThe Act on the Improvement of Removals was introduced by the coalition government in October 2023.\nIt wasvoted through parliament on 18 January 2024 and entered into force on 27 February 2024. The\nreform involves changes in the legal framework for detention, among other reforms. More specifically:\n\n       v Asylum applicants can now be detained if grounds for detention apply at the time when they\n         lodged their application. Before the reform, asylum seekers could only be detained in cases where\n         they lodged the asylum request from within detention.1170 This is relevant notably for cases where\n         persons file a subsequent application in order to avoid imminent removal.\n       v For pre-removal detention to be ordered, it is sufficient that the removal can take place within 6\n         months, instead of 3.1171\n       v The grounds for pre-removal extension are extended to cases where persons entered legally,\n         visa free or with a Schengen visa, and then overstayed their period for legal stay.1172\n       v The maximum duration of custody pending removal increases from 10 days to 28 days.1173\n       v The grounds for detention to enforce cooperation will be expanded, so that this form of detention\n         can also be orders in cases of persons who do not cooperate in the establishment of their\n         identity1174\n       v Detainees who are not yet represented by a lawyer will be provided with a lawyer by the court1175\n\n\n\n\n1170\n         Federal Government, Draft Bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung\n         der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs, 14;\n         Section 14(3) Asylum Act.\n1171\n         Federal Government, Draft Bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung\n         der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs, 11;\n         Section 62 (3) Residence Act.\n1172\n         Federal Government, Draft Bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung\n         der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs, 10,\n         46; new Section 62d Residence Act.\n\n\n                                                       177\n\nv Minors and families will not be detained “in principle”, whereas previously they could be detained\n         “only in exceptional cases and only for as long as it is adequate considering the well-being of the\n         child.’1176\n       v The new law foresees a possibility for authorities to file a complaint against the refusal by courts\n         to order detention1177\n\nThe Act also changes the authorities’ competences for enforcing removals:\n   v Exact timing of removals can no longer be announced, unless they involve families with children\n        under 12 years of age.1178 Previously, the date of removals had to be announced to persons who\n        had been holding a tolerated status for at least one year.\n   v In enforcing removals, authorities will be able to search not only the room of the person to be\n        deported, but also other rooms in a reception centre.1179 It will also be easier for authorities to\n        enter reception centres during the night to apprehend a person.1180\n   v Search competences of the authorities are being extended to that the living space and personal\n        belongings of persons can be searched in order to seize documents or electronic devices than\n        can help establish their identity or nationality or to establish whether a removal is possible. In\n        cases of ‘imminent danger’, a search no longer requires a court order.1181\n\nThe reform also tightens rules around the enforcement of entry bans and extends possibilities for the\nexpulsion of persons with a criminal conviction.1182\n\nAccording to the government, the aim of the reform was to facilitate the enforcement of removals by\nremoving ‘obstacles’ amid rising numbers of asylum seekers. The government estimates that the reform\nwill increase the number of returns by about 5%.1183 The reform was heavily criticised by NGOs for leading\nto a brutalisation of returns and for drastically reducing rights of non-nationals by heavily extending the\ngrounds for detention, including for asylum seekers. In addition, stakeholders criticised that they were\nonly given 2 days to comment on the proposals.1184 The Parliament Committee on Home Affairs and\nCommunity included some changes in the government’s draft, including the granting of a lawyer and the\nrule that minors and children are not to be detained in principle.\n\n\n\n1176\n         Deutscher Bundestag, Recommendation for a resolution and report of the Committee on Home Affairs and\n         Community (4th Committee) on the Federal Government's draft bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf\n         eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in\n         German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs, 7; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "As of 31 December 2023, asylum seekers with pending applications could generally be detained only in specified situations, including when they applied after already being detained or when they were subject to an entry ban and posed a significant security danger or had criminal convictions; NGOs criticised the latter provision as lacking safeguards for vulnerable groups and a proper EU-law basis.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.5", "3B.7", "4B.4"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The legal situation is expressly stated as of 31 December 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The relevant detention provision had been introduced in 2020 and was administered through federal and Länder institutions; the source does not attribute its design specifically to Scholz.", "limitations": ["The source reports NGO legal criticism rather than a final judicial invalidation.", "The provision predates the target year."], "start_unit": 179, "end_unit": 180, "locator": "page 179 through page 180", "exact_excerpt": "Legal situation in 2023\n\n Changes following the Act on the Improvement of Removals 2024:\n       v   Additional possibility to detain asylum applicants: asylum applicants can now be detained if\n           grounds for detention apply at the time when they lodged their application. Before the reform,\n           asylum seekers could only be detained in cases where they lodged the asylum request from\n           within detention.1185\n\nThe legal situation as of 31 December 2023 is described below.\n\nAs of 31 December 2023, according to the law, there are two possibilities for the detention of asylum\nseekers whose application is still pending. The first relates to asylum applications which are lodged by\npeople who are already in detention, in particular those:\n   v In pre-trial detention;\n   v In prison (following a conviction for a criminal or other offence); or\n   v In detention pending removal (Abschiebungshaft).1186\n\nAn asylum application lodged after a foreigner has been detained for the purpose of removal does not\nalways lead to release from detention, as detention is legally possible under certain circumstances.\nHowever, it has to be noted that detention pending removal, ordered solely on the grounds of illegal border\ncrossing, is in itself not a sufficient reason to uphold such detention in case an asylum application is\nlodged. In addition, the authorities have to prove that there are further reasons for the prolongation of\ndetention, such as a risk of absconding or an illegal stay for a duration of one month. The lodging of a\nsubsequent or second application also does not preclude the ordering of detention unless the BAMF has\ndecided to open another asylum procedure. 1187\n\nThe second possibility for detention during the asylum procedure was introduced in 2020 and relates to\npersons who are subject to an entry ban and present ‘a significant danger to their own or others’ lives, or\nto internal security’ or have been convicted for criminal offences, including asylum seekers (Section 62c\nResidence Act). According to the government, the provision is meant to allow for the detention of persons\nwho are obliged to leave the country and who file an asylum application.1188 NGOs such as PRO ASYL\nand the Federal Association for Unaccompanied Minors heavily criticised the new provision as it contains\nno safeguards for vulnerable groups and lacks a proper legal basis in the grounds for detention as\nprovided by the EU Reception Conditions Directive.1189\n\nIf the lodging of an asylum application does not lead to release from detention, a detained person may be\nkept in detention until the BAMF has decided upon the case, but for a maximum of four weeks after the\nasylum request has been submitted to the BAMF.1190 Detention may even be upheld beyond that period\nif another country has been requested to admit or re-admit the foreigner on the basis of European law,\ni.e. the Dublin Regulation, or if the application for international protection has been rejected as\ninadmissible or as manifestly unfounded.1191\n\n\n1185\n       Federal Government, Draft Bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung\n       der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs, 14;\n       Section 14(3) Asylum Act.\n1186\n       Friederike Haberstroh, Detention and Alternatives to Detention. Pre-removal detention (Abschiebungshaft) (including Dublin removal)\n\n Changes following the Act on the Improvement of Removals 2024:\n       v For pre-removal detention to be ordered, it is sufficient that the removal can take place within\n         6 months, instead of 3.1192\n       v The grounds for pre-removal extension are extended to cases where persons entered legally,\n         visa free or with a Schengen visa, and then overstayed their period for legal stay1193\n\nPre-removal detention is ordered to secure removal to the country of origin or to a third country (usually\nin the form of a Dublin transfer). It can only be ordered for asylum seekers in the situations described\nabove. The German Constitution provides that detention may only be ordered by a judge. The responsible\nauthorities may only take a person into custody if there is reason to believe that this person is trying to\nabscond to avoid removal and if a judge cannot be requested to issue a detention order beforehand. In\nsuch cases, the detention order must be subsequently obtained from a court as soon as possible.\n\nA judge may issue a detention order as ‘preparatory detention’ (Vorbereitungshaft) in cases of persons\nwho have been expelled (usually following a criminal conviction) and in cases of persons who have been\ngiven a removal order on the grounds that they pose a risk to national security.1194 In most cases, however,\na detention order is issued for the purpose of ‘securing the removal’ (Sicherungshaft). This type of\ndetention is defined in Section 62(3) of the Residence Act.\n\nThis provision underwent a major amendment in August 2019 as part of the so-called Second Act for an\nimproved enforcement of the obligation to leave the country (Zweites Gesetz zur besseren Durchsetzung\nder Ausreisepflicht, also known as the ‘Orderly Return Act’/Geordnete-Rückkehr-Gesetz). Section 62(3)\nof the Residence Act now states that a foreigner shall be placed in detention pending removal ‘if:1195\n     v there is a risk of absconding;\n     v the foreigner is required to leave the country on account that they entered the territory unlawfully;\n        or\n     v a removal order has been issued pursuant to Section 58a [against persons who have been\n        expelled or who have been found to pose a risk to national security] but is not immediately\n        enforceable’.\n\nHowever, detention remains lawful only when removal cannot be ensured by other, less severe means.1196\nAuthorities have discretion to refrain from ordering detention if the person credibly demonstrates that they\ndo not intend to evade the removal.1197 The detention order is unlawful in cases where it is clear that the\nremoval cannot take place within 3 months for reason outside the control of the detained person.1198 This\nperiod was extended to six months for persons with a criminal conviction (unless the person is subjected\nto juvenile criminal law) with a reform that entered into force on 31 December 2022.1199 Further changes\nentered into force with the Act on the Improvement of Removals (see box above),\n\n\n\n\n1192\n        Federal Government, Draft Bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung\n        der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs, 11;\n        Section 62 (3) Residence Act.\n1193\n        Federal Government, Draft Bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung\n        der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs, 10,\n        46; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "German law required detention pending removal to be a last resort, limited to the shortest possible duration, and generally excluded minors and families with minors; nevertheless, lawyers and NGOs reported that detention was imposed too often and too easily, alternatives were rarely substantively considered by courts, and practices varied sharply between Länder, including detention for 96% of forced removals in Bavaria versus 17% in Lower Saxony in 2022.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.4", "3B.5", "3B.7", "4B.4"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The legal safeguards applied in 2023; the comparative implementation figures are for 2022 and are used as the latest cited illustration of institutional variation.", "ruler_attribution": "The federal legal framework imposed proportionality requirements, while detention decisions and use of alternatives were primarily administered by Länder authorities and courts.", "limitations": ["The comparative rates are not 2023 figures.", "The source does not provide a nationwide 2023 rate for consideration of alternatives."], "start_unit": 184, "end_unit": 185, "locator": "page 184 through page 185", "exact_excerpt": "v Failure to appear in person at the diplomatic mission or at a meeting with authorised officials of\n         the foreigner’s assumed state of origin;\n       v Failure to appear in person for a medical examination for the purpose to establishing the\n         foreigner’s ability to travel.\n\nThe maximum period foreseen for this detention ground is 14 days and is subject to a court order, which\nmeans that the authorities may not carry out short-term arrests on the basis of this provision. There is no\ninformation or case-law available as to whether this ground for detention has been implemented since it\nentered into force in August 2019. In January 2020 media reports seemed to suggest that the new\n‘detention to enforce cooperation’ had not been used yet,1211 but it was not entirely clear from these reports\nwhich type of detention they were referring to. Data from Germany’s largest detention facility in Büren\n(North Rhine Westphalia), for 2023, show that the instrument is used, but only comparatively rarely with\n12 cases over the year 2023.1212\n\n          1.4. De facto detention at the airport\n\nAsylum seekers can be apprehended and de facto detained in the transit zone of an international airport.\nAlthough they are confined within the premises of a dedicated facility for the duration of the airport\nprocedure, according to the Federal Constitutional Court, being held at the transit zone is not considered\nas detention in terms of the law.1213\n\nIn practice, the applicant receives a decision of placement in the facility. For example, persons placed in\nthe detention centre of Munich Airport receive a ‘notification of residence in the airport facility’\n(Bescheinigung für den Aufenthalt in der Flughafenunterkunft) for the purpose of the airport procedure\nunder Section 18a of the Asylum Act. This notification expressly states that this form of residence is not a\nfreedom-restrictive measure. The fiction of non-entry into the territory is maintained, even if the person\nhas been transferred to a hospital or to court. Police officers have to escort the person wherever they go\noutside the facility for the fiction to be maintained.1214\n\n       2. Alternatives to detention\n\n                                   Indicators: Alternatives to Detention\n       1. Which alternatives to detention have been laid down in the law?               Reporting duties\n                                                                                        Surrendering documents\n                                                                                        Financial guarantee\n                                                                                        Residence restrictions\n                                                                                        Other\n\n       2. Are alternatives to detention used in practice?                               Yes      No\n\nThe section on pre-removal detention in the Residence Act opens with a general clause on the principle\nof proportionality:\n        ‘Detention pending removal is not permissible if the purpose of the detention can be achieved by\n        other, less severe but equally sufficient means. The detention shall be limited to the shortest\n        possible duration. Minors and families with minors shall in principle not be taken into detention\n        awaiting removal.’1215\n\n\n1211\n         br.de,Bericht: ‚Kein Bundesland nutzt ‘Geordnete-Rückkehr-Gesetz’‘, 3. January 2020.\n1212\n         Ministry for Children, Youth, Family, Equality, Refugees and Integration of North Rhine Westphalia,\n         „Sachstandsbericht Unterbringungseinrichtung für Ausreisepflichtige (UfA) in Büren“, quarterly report available\n         on the website of the Federal State parliament: https://bit.ly/49r2hvD.\n1213\n         Federal Constitutional Court, Decision of 14 May 1996, 2 BvR 1516/93. See also Federal Supreme Court,\n         Decision V ZB 170/16, 16 March 2017, available in German at: http://bit.ly/2oRx9B4.\n1214\n         See also: ECRE, Airport procedures in Germany: Gaps in quality and compliance with guarantees, May 2019,\n         available at: https://bit.ly/2ZgTn2H.\n1215\n         Section 62(1) Residence Act.\n\n\n                                                          184\n\nIn spite of this provision, the federal law does not explicitly define alternatives to detention. Administrative\nguidelines do contain some milder measures such as reporting requirements (see below), but these do\nnot depend on there being a ground for detention and hence it is questionable whether they can be\nconsidered alternatives to detention.1216 As of 2021, some Federal States (Bremen, North Rhine-\nWestphalia, Lower Saxony, Brandenburg and Schleswig-Holstein) have regulated the use of\nalternatives to detention in decrees.1217 Lawyers and NGOs have frequently criticised that detention\npending removal is imposed by the responsible local courts ‘too often and too easily’ and a high number\nof detention orders were overturned by higher courts upon appeal.1218 In court decisions, alternatives to\ndetention are rarely discussed substantially,1219 and some authorities have been found to always request\nthe maximum duration of three months without laying out how far this is necessary given the preparations\nfor removal.1220 The practice in resorting to detention also differs widely between Federal States: by way\nof example, in 2022 in Lower Saxony detention was only ordered in 134 cases compared to 789 forced\nremovals (17%), whereas in Bavaria, 1,966 persons were detained for a total of 2,046 forced removals\n(i.e., 96%).1221 The share of detention orders in relation to the overall number of removals has increased\nfrom around 10% in 2015 to around 28% in 2020.1222 However, this might also be related to the fact that\nin 2015 and 2016, the majroty of forced returns were to Western Balkan states, where returns have been\ncomparatively fast and frictionless.1223\n\nAmong the available alternatives is the ‘geographical restriction’ which normally applies to asylum seekers\nfor a period of 3 months and can be re-imposed if ‘concrete measures to end the foreigner’s stay are\nimminent’ (see Freedom of Movement).1224 The law also contains a general provision according to which\n‘further conditions and sanctions’ may be imposed on foreigners who are obliged to leave the country.1225\nIn particular, these sanctions may consist of reporting duties, the obligation to reside in a specific place\nor to be home during night-time, but also of an obligation to consult a counselling service for returnees.1226\nPassports of foreigners obliged to leave the country can be confiscated.1227 The authorities may also ask\nforeigners who are obliged to leave the country to deposit a security to cover the costs of a possible\n\n\n\n\n1216\n       Friederike Haberstroh, Detention and Alternatives to Detention. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "At Pforzheim detention facility, only one of two social workers was reportedly present during the latter half of 2023, no psychological support was available, medical-care access was disputed, and a civil-society round table created in 2023 excluded staff working in the facility and the Refugee Council.", "question_ids": ["3B.2", "3B.4", "3B.7", "6B.2", "6B.4", "8B.5"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The staffing and consultation findings specifically concern 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The facility was administered by Baden-Württemberg authorities; the source does not show federal direction by Scholz.", "limitations": ["The evidence comes primarily from Caritas and the Refugee Council rather than a federal inspection.", "It is a single-facility finding."], "start_unit": 196, "end_unit": 197, "locator": "page 196 through page 197", "exact_excerpt": "Pforzheim, Baden-Württemberg: According to the Federal State government, detainees in Pforzheim\ncan move around freely within the facilities’ accommodation and ‘leisure’ areas and are allowed to use\nmobile phones.1300 Two social workers are present in the facility, however de-facto only one was present\nover the latter half of 2023, according to the NGO Caritas, which provides external support to detainees\nthrough visits (see Access to detention facilities).1301 The Refugee Council of Baden-Württemberg\nhighlighted in 2019 that medical care had not always been guaranteed. For example, a priest had\norganised an urgent appointment at an ophthalmologist for a detainee, but the person concerned had not\nbeen allowed to leave the facility for this appointment.1302 According to Caritas, information on the\navailability of medical care cannot be verified as NGOs and support groups do not have contact to medical\nprofessionals working with detainees.1303 While the State government that took office in 2021 pledged\nsome improvements, they have only partially been adopted so far. By way of example, a “round table”\nwas set up in 2023 to exchange information between authorities in charge of detention and civil society.\nHowever, on the side of the civil society, no staff member who works in the detention facility is involved in\nthis discussion format, nor is the Refugee Council part of it. 1304 The promise to set up and make available\nseparate premises for full-time and voluntary staff and pastoral care has been only partially implemented\nthus far, according to Caritas: such a room is available in principle, but not yet fully operational as of\nJanuary 2024 due to questions of financing. When ready, the room will be used not only for independent\ncounselling but also for BAMF interviews in case of asylum requests filed during detention and for pastoral\ncare. 1305 No psychological support is available in the facility, Furthermore, as in many other detention\nfacilities in Germany, special rooms for detention with heightened security measures exist and detainees\nare placed there “too often”, e.g., if there is an assumed risk of suicide attempts before the planed\nremoval.1306\n\nHof, Bavaria: The detention centre in Hof opened on 26 October 2021 and has a capacity of 150 places.\nIt is administrated by the prison in Hof but separated from it ‘through structural and organisational\nmeasures’.1307 16 of the 150 places are foreseen for female detainees, and 4 places are suitable for\npersons with disabilities, according to the Ministry of Justice. As of October 2022, 54 law enforcement\nofficials and 20 social workers, psychologists, chaplains, and medical staff worked at the facility.1308\nAccording to the Ministry of justice, detainees have access to a range of leisure facilities including sports,\nand each room has a TV with access to international channels. Leisure activities are offered by the social\nservices present in the facilities. According to press reports, detainees can be outside their rooms between\n9 am and 7 pm.1309\n\nEichstätt, Bavaria: As of September 2023, the detention facility employs a total of 52 staff members, four\nof which are social workers and two psychologists.1310 Following a fact-finding mission conducted in April\n\n1300\n       State Ministry Baden-Württemberg, Lorek zu Besuch in Abschiebungshafteinrichtung Pforzheim, 22 July\n       2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/4byGSCd.\n1301\n       Information provided by by the Caritasverband Karlsruhe e.
